# Changelog

All notable changes to this project should be documented in this file.

The format is intentionally simple:

- `Added` for new features
- `Changed` for behavior or workflow changes
- `Fixed` for bug fixes
- `Docs` for README, guide, or release-note changes

## [Unreleased]

### Added
- **New Item Studio now uses a guided full-width workflow and a resident Effects workspace.** The old left rail is replaced by a clickable, keyboard-accessible seven-step header and a stable Back / `Step N of 7` / Continue footer; the calculated item state remains available through each step's tooltip and accessibility text. Step 5 has separate Perks and Effects tabs. Effects lists every shipped stem with a neutral mechanical label, exact authority text, fixed category filters and a `Loop` or `One-shot` badge, then stages selection, transform, colour and four look multipliers until **Apply placement** commits them as one draft change. Its three resizable panes provide the real Move, Rotate, Scale, Front, Side, Top, Frame, Pause, anchor, bounds, fit, backdrop, character, colour and logarithmic look controls around one resident .NET/Vortice renderer; rapid package changes are serialized latest-wins, preserve the camera, and retain the old package until the correlated renderer acknowledgement. Numeric placement remains available with an explicit unreviewed-placement acknowledgement when the renderer cannot start.
- **New Item Studio now preflights visual effects against the actual prefabs each item will own.** Effect planning no longer decides support from an equipment-type name: it dry-runs the real `EffectComponent` graft against every cloned prefab and refuses the whole plan with the exact failing path rather than writing a partial item. Compatible armour, accessory and other equipment families can therefore use the same visual-effect path as weapons, while shared borrowed prefabs remain untouched; an imported item's explicitly cloned sheathed prefabs receive the effect as well.
- **New Item Studio can turn selected faces of an imported model into new parts directly through Mesh Editor.** The Model & Placement step opens its current source mesh in the resident editor with Faces as the selection target; select with Click, Brush, Rectangle or Lasso, then choose **Create Part from Selection** directly in Selection. Returning to New Item Studio and choosing **Use Mesh Editor changes** drains the current stroke, captures one stable revision off the UI thread, rebuilds the textured source, exposes the uniquely named appended part to per-part Glow, and invalidates any placement build made from the older geometry. The operation moves existing faces from one source part and preserves their imported vertex/material data; it is not an arbitrary knife cut and does not generate capped cut surfaces.
- **New Item Studio: effect placement now offers Invert orbit X and Invert orbit Y inside Preview.** The two switches apply immediately to the resident viewport and use the same saved preferences as Model Preview Settings, so someone who prefers either camera axis reversed can choose it where the effect is being placed and keep that choice for the other resident previews.
- **New Item Studio: the effect can be rotated on the weapon, in the viewport and in the numbers.** The placement dialog gains a **Rotate** tool between Move and Scale -- the resident viewport's placement gizmo has carried the rotate rings all along; the dialog simply never offered them -- and three rotation boxes beside the offsets, Euler degrees about the weapon's own axes, x then y then z, the order the viewport composes. A ring drag and a typed number share one state, and with the game's character on screen the ring the reader drags is the scene's while the numbers stay the item's: the turn crosses the character attachment by conjugation, the same path the offsets already crossed, exact because the effect's scale is uniform. The turn is written into the grafted `_offsetTransform` as the quaternion of the same rotation the viewport showed. The transform's rotation field is proven in shipped data -- the spear's own effect carries a 0.7 scale and a quarter turn about z -- while the studio's own written turn awaits an in-game fit check, and the step's existing warning says as much. The anchor also grows a small axis triad in the gizmo's own axis colours, because an octahedron is the same shape from every side and a rotation was invisible on it.

- **New Item Studio: the Glow choice shows in the Model step's viewport as you make it.** Ticking a part, picking the colour or changing the strength now lights that part in the step's preview, and in an icon captured from it, the way the shipped item will glow: the export writes each glowing material as a solid intensity map times a colour times a number, and the resident renderer draws the same three values sent as live parameter overrides, so the preview and the game agree by construction. An un-ticked part goes back to the emissive the imported model brought, or to none. Two limits are the preview's own: the renderer normalises strength against the brightest shipped emitter, so strengths from 4 up to the cap of 20 all read as full brightness, and it draws no bloom, so a strong glow reads as a bright surface rather than a halo.

### Changed
- **New Item Studio keeps Model, Icon and Placement visible together.** Step 3 is now **Model & Placement** and uses two balanced columns instead of Model / Placement / Icon tabs: the left column keeps an independently scrolling model form above always-visible icon choices, while the right column keeps placement controls, live operation status and the resident preview visible. Imported-material, sheathed/holstered, template-physics, Glow and texture-flip controls remain with the model, and the redundant **Import tips** block remains removed.
- **New Item Studio puts Output into two working columns.** Build the plan and its change review now stay together on the left, while every write, install and overlay action stays in a dedicated right column so the final step uses wide windows without turning the workflow into one long vertical stack.
- **New Item Studio keeps higher-rank perk names clean.** Perks ending in II or III no longer repeat the `— experimental` suffix in Available or Selected lists; unproven standalone perks still carry the marker, and the detail pane keeps the evidence warning.
- **New Item Studio is compact without hiding its working controls.** The seven-step navigator is one 46 px row with 22 px numbered circles and an animated progress line, and the duplicate page-title strips are gone. Perks use searchable Available/Selected lists instead of a screen-filling combo; Effects use regular-weight 24 px table rows with marker, Effect, Type and approximate Size columns, while the exact stem stays in details/tooltips. Every Qt combo popup elsewhere is capped at ten rows and 40% of the active screen.
- **New Item Studio shows imported geometry first and keeps Apply responsive.** The resident renderer now accepts a geometry-only package, then upgrades a copy with canonical materials without exporting the geometry again or resetting the camera. On the 52,529-vertex, 9-texture Frostmourne fixture, first geometry measured 1.12 s and the dense two-role textured workspace 6.29 s, compared with the previous 5.31 s model-only and 10.35 s model-plus-reference preparations. Apply uses the native affine path, vectorizes external material factors, deduplicates identical submesh synthesis, reports cancellable phases, and remains off the GUI thread; three repeated dense builds improved from a 4.05 s median to 3.49 s with the same rebuilt SHA-256, while a 10 ms GUI heartbeat peaked at 75.8 ms and the spinner advanced 114 frames.
- **New Item Studio makes automatic and manual identifiers visibly different.** Item key and Model stem now stay read-only behind an explicit **Manual** switch while the planner owns them; choosing Manual starts from the same next-free key or template-shaped stem the plan would allocate, and switching back removes the override. Internal names and custom stems enforce their actual character sets and 64-character limits in the editor, number grouping makes custom item keys readable, and a compact state icon beside each identifier points to its collision or format message in Checks. The allocation ranges and collision policy are unchanged.
- **Mesh Editor opens an archive mesh roughly three times faster.** Measured on `cd_phw_00_nude_00_0001_damian.pac` (27,480 vertices in the editable-plus-reference scene) on a machine running a game at the same time, back to back against the previous build. The resident helper went from 14.5-16.1 s to 3.8-4.0 s from launch to `ready`. Three causes in the helper, each with its own fix. The tool flanks are AutoSize table layouts nested four deep and the layout engine measured every flat button several times per pass: roughly 200,000 preferred-size queries for 73 buttons before the window ever showed, each re-laying out the caption from scratch; the button now caches its preferred size per constraint until its text, font, padding or size limits change. Every section, re-parent and rail move during the hidden startup laid out the whole flank again (332 table layouts, most measuring unchanged content); the build phases now suspend layout across the entire subtree and settle it in one closing pass per phase. And the window was built at the constructor's default size and resized to the host afterwards, which re-laid out the finished tool tree at a new width (3.5-3.9 s on its own); it now takes the host's size before a single child exists. The Python side of the open also shed work: loading the mesh no longer builds the per-vertex asset view twice (once for status, once for the no-op roundtrip), 2.4 s to 0.4 s; opening the edit session and cloning the working mesh share the immutable asset snapshot instead of deep-copying tens of thousands of frozen records, 0.9 s to 0.5 s and 0.4 s to 0.2 s; and the skinning summary that the asset, the mesh OBJ export and the scene OBJ export each recomputed over every bone row is computed once per part content and reused. Layout counters and phase marks ride along in the helper's `startup_timing` event.
- **New Item Studio: the Combat stats and prices step reads as one ladder and one shop price, and its buttons name their targets.** The ladder group is titled as what it is, one row per enhancement level with the raw stats and the price at each, and the shop price and stack size sit under their own title. Selecting a cell now shows three short lines under the grid, the cell, how it compares with the template (value, difference and percentage; or unchanged; or an addition where the template has no value), and what the game's own equipment carries for that stat, amber when the value is far outside that range, instead of one long sentence that repeated the step's own caveat. Edited cells turn blue on a blue wash the moment they are committed rather than at the next refill, and a typo puts the last valid value back into that one cell instead of re-laying the whole step. Added levels are labelled as added in the row header and can be taken back with **Remove the last added level**; **Add a level** is disabled at the cap rather than ignoring the click. Under Advanced, the exact-value button names the stat column it writes (**Set Attack (DDD) at every level**) and the remove button names the added column it drops, so neither needs a status-bar message to explain a refusal. **Set editable prices to 1** is now **Set all prices here to 1**.
- **Mesh Editor: the duplicate Skeleton and Pose buttons beside the part-pick status are gone.** The strip under the resident viewport carried its own Skeleton and Pose buttons, duplicating the Character Preview palette group and the Rig panel: three controls emitted the same pose-preview command, and two opened the same panel. The strip now ends at the part-pick and performance readouts. The one thing only the strip's Skeleton button did, re-requesting the .NET/Vortice preview so the skeleton overlay metadata refreshes, moved onto the palette's Skeleton button, which now opens the Rig panel and refreshes the overlay in one click.
- **Mesh Editor now opens archive meshes directly as a mesh-only authoring workspace.** Archive Browser keeps one **Open in Mesh Editor** route and no longer exposes mesh replacement, import-preview, texture/material, recolour, glow, swap, or Texture Editor handoffs; Model Library sends downloaded models to New Item Studio instead. The resident Edit Mesh controls are visible immediately with read-only **Solid (Textured)** review, fixed-pivot whole-object transforms, draft recovery, same-source editable-package interchange, atomic mesh export, loose/DMM mesh-only packages, and confirmed overlay install/restore with backup and rollback. Static replacement implementation remains archived for compatibility, dedicated texture tools are unchanged, and shipped PAMT/PAZ archives are never patched by the new outputs.

### Fixed
- **Archive Browser, Mesh Editor, and New Item Studio now combine source colours with the same material detail.** Archive Browser and Model Library pushed a stale `1.0` tone gamma over the resident renderer's source-calibrated `0.92`, while Archive Browser pre-combined a material that the canonical package compiler then synthesized again and New Item Studio and Mesh Editor used only the latter at a private `0.028` relief strength. The default profiles now agree at `0.92`, complete untouched `1.0` profiles migrate without replacing custom tuning, and every surface uses one canonical synthesis pass with the combiner's standard `0.04` relief contribution. Source sRGB hues such as gold and dye colours therefore remain independent from linear normal, height, roughness, metallic, specular, and occlusion response.
- **New Item Studio Effects now keeps the Glow chosen in Model & Placement.** The Model step sent its per-part colour and strength only to its own resident preview, while Effects built a separate package from the import's original emissive values and kept that stale package when the step was revisited. Effects now carries the current Glow into its copied material authority and refreshes the existing placement preview on entry, without mutating the reusable imported model.
- **Mesh Editor now has a Close control for the resident Mesh Edit Session.** The control stays at the far edge of the strip beneath the viewport, asks before discarding geometry changes that have not been exported or built, and returns to Mesh Editor's empty state through the existing nonblocking worker, renderer and session teardown path.
- **New Item Studio applies a clicked template immediately.** An explicit mouse selection no longer waits through the 180 ms keyboard-navigation settle window before rebuilding the dependent steps. Rapid arrow-key movement still commits only the final row, retaining the protection against rebuilding once per row passed.
- **New Item Studio now uses compact-window space without nested page scrolling or repeated state.** At 1280×720 in the graphite theme, Distribution keeps its optional group picker inside the page and Output keeps plan review readable in its own editor; both now measure zero outer vertical scroll while expanding again at 1600×900. Custom Perks lists grow with the workspace instead of remaining two short 210 px boxes. Model & Placement shows sheathed/holstered and template-physics choices only when the selected template can use them, and model texture loading appears once in the pinned operation bar rather than again below the preview. Effects keeps `No effect` as the single empty-state authority, hides empty compatibility/stem lines, and separates numeric suffixes in normalized names (`Fire 1`, not `Fire1`).
- **New Item Studio Model & Placement no longer collapses into repeated `QObject::eventFilter()` errors.** The app-wide combo-popup limiter forwarded Qt's internal non-`QObject` layout items into the `QObject.eventFilter` base binding during localization, recursively interrupting the preview host before its controller and status widgets existed. The limiter now returns the standard unhandled result directly for those events while retaining the ten-row popup cap, and the exact packaged `QWidgetItem` regression is part of the smoke gate.
- **Mesh Editor Face Brush responds immediately across short strokes, and Create Part no longer races the visible selection.** Projection data is prewarmed off the input path, retained until geometry/camera/visibility state changes, and depth is prepared only for touched tiles; a first dab queues against a correlated cold build instead of constructing the whole cache on the UI thread. The production D3D11 soak selected faces in 12/12 short gestures with 7.16 ms input p95, 31.26 ms maximum host-heartbeat gap, one cold build, 21.26 ms cold visible result and 2.70 ms warm first-dab result. **Create Part from Selection** now waits for provisional Brush authority, requires Faces from one source part, and reveals the uniquely named appended row with its moved-face count.
- **New Item Studio Effects now renders the same imported materials as Model & Placement before and after Apply placement.** After Apply, the placement preview selected the rebuilt PAC's borrowed template wrapper as material authority—in the reported Frostmourne case, `CD_PHM_01_Guard_0038` with 27 template bindings—instead of the still-live import's `Material` row and its three source PBR maps, producing a dark synthesized surface. Effects now keeps the live import source as its placed preview authority; the rebuilt result remains output authority and is only a preview fallback when no source survives. The placement tint pass remains restricted to the synthetic character, anchor, reach and axis helpers, so item alpha, metallic, roughness, texture resources and parameters stay unchanged.
- **New Item Studio effect-preview cleanup is now ownership-checked.** Closing or superseding a placement preview can remove only one direct `package_*` child beneath that workspace's resolved output root; the root itself, `.`, synthetic paths and unrelated folders are refused, and the regression is part of the smoke gate.
- **New Item Studio: the weapon in effect placement keeps the imported textures shown in Model & Placement.** The placement package correctly detected those textures, then turned the weapon into the character's hand frame through a dataclass copy that kept ordinary mesh fields but dropped the runtime preview texture paths and material parameters; material synthesis consequently received geometry without the files it had just found and drew the neutral fallback. The turn now preserves the complete preview-material metadata before packaging, so resolvable imported textures reach the resident viewport while the character reference is enabled.
- **Mesh Editor no longer fails to load a mesh on a busy machine.** Opening an archive mesh could end in an endless restart loop: the resident .NET/Vortice helper was killed ten seconds after launch, relaunched, and killed again, eleven times in one session, with every kill logged as `ready_watchdog_expired` and "Stale .NET mesh editor session id". The helper was never hung. Its startup simply took longer than the host's fixed ten-second readiness deadline whenever the machine was loaded (measured with a game running: 11-16 s from launch to `ready` for a 27k-vertex character), because the deadline covered the whole hidden window build, and a helper still inside its form constructor pumps nothing the host could read as life. Two changes. The helper now reports `startup_progress` at every startup phase, and the host re-arms the ten-second deadline on each report, up to ninety seconds per launch, so a slow open is a slow open rather than a loop; a helper that genuinely stops reporting still fails, just later. And the helper publishes a `startup_timing` summary (phase marks, milliseconds, window handles, layout counters) just before `ready`, recorded in the Mesh Editor protocol trail, so the next slow open can be read from the log instead of guessed at.
- **Every resident viewport: a horizontal drag now turns the model with the pointer.** The Mesh Editor, the Archive Browser and Model Library previews and the New Item Studio placement and icon viewports share the helper's camera, and its horizontal orbit ran against the hand: drag right and the side of the model facing you slid left, while the vertical orbit and both pan axes already followed the pointer, so one axis of every camera gesture felt inverted (reported from the New Item Studio placement viewport, where the plain left button orbits). The helper's yaw now runs the way Blender and Maya orbit: the near side follows the pointer on both axes. **Invert orbit X** in Model Preview Settings still reverses it for anyone who preferred the old feel. `CaptureCameraParityProof` gains an `orbit_follows_pointer` gate that drives the viewport's own move handler on a synthetic mesh and reads where the vertex drawn nearest the reader lands, by the renderer's depth; `tests/test_dotnet_capture_camera_parity.py` asserts it.
- **New Item Studio: the Combat stats and prices step no longer lags on every edit.** Two causes, measured on the studio's own test package (a 2-level, 3-column ladder). The tab refreshed the rail summary from the stats tables' `itemChanged`, which Qt emits once per cell a refill writes, and every refresh validated the whole draft twice, once for the Identity checks and once for the rail: one rebuild of that small ladder ran the summary 10 times and validated 20 times, an added level 14 and 28, an added stat column 17 and 34, and typing one number 2 and 4. And every validation rebuilt the collision context from scratch, frozensets over every item key and name, every StringInfo text, every part-prefab stem, every English localisation key and every store's stock, which on the real archives is the whole 1.7-million-entry snapshot, so the cost per validation scaled with the game and the count scaled with the grid. Now one edit refreshes the rail once, after the draft changed, through the plan invalidation every edit already raised; a refill runs it zero times, with the tables' signals blocked while they are filled; one validation serves both the checks and the rail; and the context is built once per template and kept on the read-only snapshot, so a validation is set lookups. The rail also used to read the grid before an added column had been inserted, which could count the new column's values as price edits until the next refresh; the refill now precedes the invalidation.
- **Every resident viewport (Mesh Editor, the Archive Browser preview, New Item Studio's placement and icon views): the light stays where it is set as the view turns.** The renderer lights surfaces, and sorts transparent parts, through the camera's `World` matrix, while it draws geometry through its `WorldViewProjection`; the interactive camera built those two as separate hand-written matrices with different rotations (`RotX(pitch) * RotY(yaw)` against the projection's `RotY(-yaw) * RotX(pitch)`), and folded the pan in before the rotation instead of after. They agreed at yaw 0 only, which is where every framing starts, so the lit side matched the surface on arrival and drifted against it as the view was orbited: at yaw 90 a light set to come from the left lit the right. `World` is now the view frame the projection draws in (the camera's right, up and forward as its axes, the pan applied after the rotation), identical at yaw 0 with no pan, so no baseline taken at a default framing changes; orbited and panned views now light from the same side the settings say. The capture parity proof holds `World * projection` to the screen's matrix within 0.05 px at seven views, checks the rotation is proper, and pins the yaw-0 matrix to the one the lighting was tuned on.
- **New Item Studio and Model Library: an icon captured from the viewport shows the view on screen, at any angle.** *Take the icon from this view* (and the Model Library's icon capture) came out at a different angle from the preview whenever the view had been orbited, and matched only the overhead framing a weapon loads with, which is what made it look random. The resident renderer aimed the capture with the camera's `World` matrix and a fresh projection, while the screen draws from the camera's `WorldViewProjection`; the interactive camera builds those two by hand and they do not encode the same rotation, nor fold the pan in at the same point, so the capture agreed with the screen at yaw 0 only. Measured on an 800 x 600 viewport: at yaw 30, pitch -45 the same point landed 142 px from where the screen drew it, at yaw 90 with a pan 211 px, and a vertical pan at the overhead pitch 79 px (nearly dropped). The capture is now the screen's own matrix with x and y rescaled for the capture's size, which is exactly the camera re-made at the scaled zoom and pan, and for the visual-audit camera (consistent by construction) exactly what it was before. A GPU-free helper proof (`--headless-capture-camera-parity`, `tests/test_dotnet_capture_camera_parity.py`, in `mesh-unit`) runs the real capture camera over both constructors at seven views and holds them to 0.05 px.
- **New Item Studio: the effect preview stops clipping to flat white, stops slicing hard lines through the blade, and keeps the effect's own brightness balance.** Four rendering causes, each its own fix. Additive sprites accumulated into the 8-bit target, so a dozen overlapping particles clipped to a white column with no hue left; the game accumulates in HDR and tone-maps, and the preview now uses screen blending (source premultiplied, `s*(1-d)+d`), the closed form of that curve, which approaches white asymptotically and keeps the fire's orange in the bright core. A sprite crossing the blade was cut by the depth test along a hard pixel line; the particle pass now binds the depth buffer read-only, samples it, and fades each sprite over the last few centimetres before geometry -- the placement camera is orthographic, so NDC depth is linear and the fade is exact in metres -- and on a device that refuses the read-only view the fade simply stays off. Every emitter was normalised by its own colour peak, so a dim ember emitter drew as bright as the blazing core beside it; the whole effect now shares one normaliser, with emitters whose colours could not be read at all (drawn on their own sprite) exempt. And alpha-blended emitters drew in list order, so a near smoke puff could sit under a far one; their particles are now laid out back to front. The particle pump also steps at 16 ms rather than 33, since it only invalidates and the simulation follows measured wall time.
- **Mesh Editor and placement viewports: combined rotations compose in one order everywhere.** The helper carried two Euler conventions: the live-drag path and the host's own matrix compose x, then y, then z, while two fallback paths (the editable model matrix without an authoritative frame, and the editable authority matrix) went through `CreateFromYawPitchRoll`, which is z, then x, then y. The two agree on any single-axis turn and silently disagree the moment two axes are combined -- a placement could draw one way during the drag and another at rest. Both fallback paths now compose through the same x-y-z matrix as the drag path, which is also the order the New Item Studio's new effect rotation writes into the game data.
- **Mesh Editor: Solid (Textured) now waits for the archive texture lookup instead of failing on a model the Archive Browser previews textured.** The shell defers its path/basename lookup build until something needs it, and the Mesh Editor's material context resolution ran against the empty maps that deferral leaves behind: every embedded material name then reported "no direct visible DDS match" and the Preview mode selector snapped back to Faces (No Textures), even though the same call with the built lookup resolves every binding (proven on `cd_phw_00_nude_00_0001_damian.pac`: three of three bindings with the lookup, zero without). The resolution now asks the shell to build the lookup, the same call the Archive Browser preview makes, and retries when it lands, while the textured-view watchdog keeps the request alive. Every terminal textured-view failure also sends its reason to the resident helper's status line, which previously read "Loading textures in the resident viewport..." forever while the only explanation went to the main window's status bar.
- **Edit Mesh sculpt strokes stop paying whole-mesh costs on every pointer sample, and a non-smooth Grab no longer snaps at release.** A captured session put numbers on the stall: before Subdivide a sculpt sample completed in 3.6 ms; after four Subdivides (13,740 to 61,178 vertices) a Grab's begin phase took 2.4 to 3.1 s, every later sample of the stroke queued behind it in the single-flight dispatcher, and the surface chased the pointer seconds late while the renderer itself held 95 to 120 FPS. Four causes are removed. The screen-brush occlusion mask was software-rasterized from every face of the mesh on every brush sample; an edit stroke now builds it once, at begin, over the whole viewport, with occlusion deliberately frozen at the begin-time geometry so the stroke's own displacement cannot flicker vertices in and out of the footprint. The selection payload and its reuse signature were built separately per apply, which serialized the whole selection twice; after a topology change cleared the reuse signature that was the full remapped selection twice on the interactive hot path, and both now come from a single build. The UI thread asked the session for its view on every stroke sample, which takes the session's export lock, so pointer events queued at the native apply's pace instead of the pointer's; it now asks once per stroke, at begin. And the Grab echo weighted its provisional surface through a private falloff copy hardcoded to smooth, so any other falloff choice snapped to a different authoritative surface at release; it now weights through the shared port of the native brush profile with the active falloff option. Each completed stroke dispatch also persists the apply's own timing breakdown (session open, native apply, C++ and serialization milliseconds) to the interaction trail, so the next slow stroke names its slow stage instead of needing a debugger.
- **Edit Mesh brush select no longer stutters in edge mode, and wires under the brush stop dropping out of the selection.** The lag had four independent causes, each a full-mesh walk on a path that runs per pointer sample: hovering in edge mode re-ray-cast every face of every submesh for each candidate wire near the cursor (the cursor ray's nearest surface is now found once per move, with face orientations memoized); applying an authoritative wire selection looked each pushed edge up by scanning the whole edge list, quadratic as the selection grew (now a dictionary the topology already built and threw away); the local paint echo had spatial buckets for vertices and faces but scanned every edge per dab (edges now share the same bucket grid); and in visible mode every dab of a coalesced native command re-rasterized every face into its own occlusion mask while each replayed dab also copied the entire request payload (one shared mask over the union of the dabs, one reusable payload envelope). Wires failing to select had three: a fast curved stroke's authoritative request covered the straight chord between throttled samples while the echo painted the curve (the request now carries the actual pointer polyline); the native depth test judged an edge by a single sample point that could sit in a z-fighting pocket of the rasterized mask (it now samples along the edge's in-brush span, and the mask cap doubled to 2048); and an edge with one endpoint outside the projectable depth window was rejected whole (the visible span is now clipped and tested). The echo also now filters by the same kind of occlusion raster the native authority uses instead of face orientation alone, so visible-mode tint no longer claims occluded geometry the authoritative result then un-selects.
- **Edit Mesh Subdivide no longer scatters splits across the mesh, cracks the surface at region borders, or snowballs into a freeze when repeated.** Three native defects compounded. The session remapped a face selection to compact face offsets after a Subdivide but read it back as source-face ids once the source map was no longer identity — which the first Subdivide itself guarantees — so the second click split every face whose ancestor id collided with an offset value, anywhere on the mesh; face-selection values are now compact offsets everywhere, with a dedicated raw-input map captured at store time for callers whose malformed face rows were dropped. A split face's unselected neighbour kept spanning the whole original edge over the new midpoint, a T-junction that cracked open as soon as anything moved it; neighbours are now stitched against the midpoints on their edges, and the face-limit check counts those children exactly before anything mutates. The post-Subdivide remap also selected every generated midpoint, adopting the bled boundary ring so the split region and the visible selection quadrupled per click — measured 9 → 61 → 255 → 1,003 → 3,939 selected vertices, now 9 → 25 → 81 → 289 → 1,089 with the split region pinned to the painted area — and a request the 200,000-face cap rejects now reports the native reason ("subdivide would exceed the per-submesh face limit…") and leaves the resident session intact instead of tearing it down as lost and forcing a recovery stall.
- **Mesh Editor output buttons now visibly react to hover, press, and unavailable states.** The five actions beneath the resident viewport are Qt tool buttons, while the app theme previously styled only push buttons, so pointer feedback fell through to a nearly flat platform default. **Run validation**, **Export Mesh File**, **Build Mod**, **Install as Overlay**, and **Restore Last Overlay Install** now use themed backgrounds and accent borders on hover and press, with a clearly dimmed disabled state; their existing validation, receipt, and safe-output gates are unchanged.
- **Mesh Editor now shows only the resident Mesh Edit Session workspace, and direct Preview Mode can load `Solid (Textured)`.** The obsolete outer Tools/Edit/UV/Rig deck, duplicate part/material inspector, and slow-frame log remain compatibility-only and are hidden from normal sessions; required validation, mesh output, overlay, and editable-package actions stay on the compact strip below the resident viewport. Direct archive sessions no longer send Preview Mode through the retired embedded-builder readiness check: their captured read-only material model is published to the resident helper, and a geometry-only Archive Browser handoff now starts the same archive material resolver off the UI thread instead of reporting that no resolver exists. The untextured fallback remains honest while binding is pending, and the acknowledged textured mode is restored without replacing geometry or editing source textures. The resident helper itself also no longer disables **Run validation**, **Export Mesh File**, **Build Mod**, or **Install as Overlay**, so those Qt-owned actions remain usable while editing. Direct load, material and output workers now return to the UI thread before teardown, and a tampered overlay receipt is rejected before any backup restore begins.

## [0.11.0-alpha.6] - 2026-08-21

### Added
- **New Item Studio: choosing a template no longer stops the window, and the effect viewport can be turned, fitted and trusted.** Four things, measured against the real archives (6,579 rows, 1.7 million entries). **The template freeze** was one lazy import: the Stats step measures what shipped equipment carries for a stat, which takes 17 ms over the corpus and imports `statistics` to do it, and an import made after PySide is loaded goes through shiboken's feature hook, which reads the source of every module it touches. That was 1.5 s, paid by the first template chosen. The snapshot worker already spends six seconds reading tables, so it takes this too: the first template went from 1,926 ms to 65 ms. The list also asked for a full five-step rebuild once per row the arrow keys passed through; it now takes the row the reader settles on, 180 ms later, and leaving the step counts as settling. **The camera** was always reachable (Alt or Ctrl orbits, Shift pans) but the placement gizmo owns the plain left button and nothing said so, which is a viewport that reads as stuck on the anchor. Right-drag is bound to orbit in the placement dialogs, and the gestures remain available in the viewport tooltip. **Fit it to the item** set the scale and left the camera framed on the twenty metres it had just fitted away, with the frame still hidden if it had dwarfed the item, so the button read as doing nothing: it now re-points the camera and shows the frame it just fitted. The scale is also kept to the same three decimals the box shows, where before the dialog held 0.034 against a box reading 0.03 and the next edit of any other field silently took the box's. **The character** in the effect viewport is now the game's whole low-detail figure or the shipped stand-in, never armour standing in for a body. Rendered offscreen, the armour fallback drew the install's median upper and lower body: a coat with a helm floating where the head should be, legs that stop above their boots, and daylight between the three. A strut figure reads as a stand-in; a torn body reads as a fault in the item.
- **New Item Studio: FBX is imported, by converting it with a Blender you point the studio at.** FBX was refused with no reason given, which reads as a gap rather than a decision, and it is the format most models on the download sites are published in. The container was never the problem: binary FBX is a typed node tree and a ninety-line probe walks one. The semantics are. The transform stack (pre- and post-rotation, pivots, the geometric offset), the layer-element mapping modes, and the axis and unit conversion in `GlobalSettings` are where an FBX arrives rotated, mirrored or a hundred times too large, and getting those right across the long tail is what Blender already does correctly. So the studio reads FBX through Blender. The Model step carries the row in the open, beside the import button: **For FBX support, Blender is required**, and **Choose blender.exe...** beside it. From then on an `.fbx`, loose or inside a zip, is converted to glTF on import and goes down the same route every other model does. Without one an FBX is refused *before* anything is read: whether a source needs Blender is answered from its name and, for a zip, its listing, so nothing is extracted, no worker starts, and the reason is on the step rather than in the window's status line. A zip that also holds a model the studio reads itself needs no Blender and imports as it always did. Nothing searches the machine and quietly uses what it finds: a Blender found here is where the file dialog opens, and the one that runs is the one you picked, so a conversion is always something you asked for and can account for when the result looks wrong. It runs `--background --factory-startup`, so no window opens and your own add-ons and preferences cannot change what comes out; a zip is extracted whole first, so the images beside the FBX are still there to be matched by name afterwards, which is what saves the many FBX files that reference no textures of their own (three of the magic sword's five bound that way). What Blender says on the way out is what the log says: the version that ran, the vertices and objects that came through, the materials and images it found, and on a failure Blender's own last line rather than "the import failed". Proven end to end on a downloaded `magic-sword.zip`: Blender 5.1.1, 1,458 vertices in one object, no materials of its own, imported at 1,996 vertices with three textures bound by name.
- **New Item Studio: the effect viewport gets a dark backdrop, a pause, and a panel in two groups.** The viewport cleared to the Mesh Editor's mid grey, which is chosen for judging a material's response, where a near-black clear lets dark leather melt into it. An additive effect is the other problem: measured on the same weapon fire, the effect stands 173 shades above a #101014 backdrop and 131 above the grey. **Backdrop** now offers Dark, Grey and Black, opens on Dark, and is remembered. **Pause** holds the simulation where it is with the particles still drawn -- hiding them answers what is under the fire, holding them answers where exactly one particle is, which a cloud in motion never lets anyone read. And the panel is two groups, **Placement** and **Preview**, with the five-row legend folded behind *What the colours mean*: fourteen controls, five legend rows and four labels in one column read as a wall.
- **New Item Studio: choose which parts of the item glow, in what colour, and how strongly.** The Model step gained a **Glow** group: the item's material parts as its model names them, a colour, and a strength (the shipped magic weapons run 1 to 10; the game's own limit is 20). A ticked part is written as `SkinnedMeshEmissive` with a solid intensity map, the chosen colour and the chosen strength; the game's emissive is one colour and one number per material, so a part either glows or it does not. Off by default, and a model that brought an emissive map of its own keeps using it.
- **New Item Studio: a Trail button that puts the effect where the game hangs this weapon's own trail.** Every one of the 48 shipped weapon socket files carries an `FX_Trail_00_Socket`, and it is the game's own answer for where a weapon's trail effect is anchored: 2 to 4 per cent from the tip on an axe or a mace, a little past it on a sword, partway along a chain weapon. The placement dialog reads it from the socket file the item's prefab names and offers it beside Hand, Middle and Tip, with the point in the tooltip. It appears only when that file was the item's own: socket files are shared between weapons, so a borrowed one would put the trail at another weapon's tip, which is a worse answer than not offering one.
- **New Item Studio: the effect is placed against the game's own character, held the way the game holds it.** The figure behind the item was a stand-in, a 1.75 m strut with the item at its hand, which answered how big an effect is but not where it would be. The dialog now reads the player rig (`character/model/1_pc/1_phm/phm_01.pab`, 434 bones), the socket file that puts `RHand_Socket` on the weapon bone, and the character's own low-detail body (798 vertices, floor to the top of the head), and stands that character in the viewport with the weapon hand at the origin. Two frames meet there and both are kept: the offsets stay the item's own, because that is what the game reads off the weapon's prefab, while the picture is the character's, because a camera has an up and a body lying at sixty degrees reads as a fault. The item and the effect's reach frame are turned into the character's frame by the attachment; an offset typed into the spin boxes is turned the same way on its path to the gizmo, and a drag is turned back before it reaches the numbers. **The attachment is not the hand socket alone**, which is the thing that is easy to get wrong and invisible to every file-level check: the Placement studio composes it as `inverse(child socket on the item) . body socket world`, and for a one-hand sword that child socket is a quarter turn about y, so a weapon hung on `RHand_Socket` by itself is held ninety degrees off. Which child socket applies is named by the item's own prefab (`_socketFileName`, and weapons share those files: sword_0039's prefab names sword_0001's), with the frame most weapons of the same kind use standing in when there is no prefab to read, and the log saying which of the two it was. Measured against the studio's own `attachment_matrix` on Kliff and a one-hand sword: equal to the sixth decimal. The character is read once per snapshot and the mating frame once per template, both off the UI thread, and an install whose archives carry no rig falls back to the stand-in figure.
- **New Item Studio: a second item can join the first one's mod folder, and the DMM folder is the one DMM mounts.** A loose mod carries whole tables, so two of them cannot both be enabled: whichever the manager mounts last owns `iteminfo.pabgb` and the other item's row is simply not there. Point the Output step at a folder that already holds a mod and it says what is in it, and **Add to the mod already in this folder** plans the next item on *those* tables, so the folder ends up holding both items in one table. It is the same thing the overlay install does when it reads the archives back between items, and it works for every manager because it stops making a second mod at all. Separately, the DMM layout is now an archive group -- `<group>/0.pamt` and `0.paz` beside a `meta/0.papgt` naming it -- rather than loose game-relative files. DMM's own mount summary counts mods as JSON, browser/file, standalone-overlay or group-replace, and a six-megabyte table belongs to the last two, which is why the loose layout did nothing there; the group is built by the same writer whose output reproduces a shipped archive byte for byte. New and not yet confirmed against DMM itself.
- **New Item Studio: the effect's particles can be taken off the item, and the reach frame is shown at a zoom that holds it.** An effect's fire is a wall of additive sprites over the thing being placed, so *Show the particles* takes it away for a moment and leaves the item, the anchor and the character; the switch travels in the same display block as the grid's (`set_effect_particles_visible`), and the helper stops the simulation rather than skipping the draw. *Show the reach* now moves the camera as well: the frame of an effect built for a boss is twenty metres across a one-metre sword, so at the item's own zoom ticking the box changed nothing anyone could see. The view now zooms out far enough to hold the frame, and back to the item when the box is cleared; the standing views keep whichever subject is being shown.
- **New Item Studio: the effect placement viewport says what everything in it is.** *Place in viewport* drew a sword, an anchor and some particles against a black background with nothing to judge any of it by. A character now stands behind the item, a figure 1.75 m tall holding the weapon in its right hand, so an effect's size reads against something known instead of against an unlabelled metre. A legend beside the viewport names each thing in the colour it is drawn: the anchor to drag, the item, the character, the reach frame, the particles. Four buttons under the viewport turn the camera to the standing views (Front, Side, Top and the three-quarter view it opens on), and **Side** is the one that shows a blade end to end, which is where an effect on a weapon is judged. Three more move the effect's origin to a named place on the item -- the hand it is held by, the middle, the far end -- instead of leaving the reader to guess which of three spin boxes runs along the blade. The character can be turned off like the reach frame, and the legend follows what is on screen.
- **New Item Studio: items already in the shipped archives can move into the overlay, and the overlay can be taken away.** Step 7 gained two more buttons. *Move installed items into the overlay* reads the oldest backup of every archive file, names every entry whose stored bytes differ from it, carries those into the overlay directory and puts the archives themselves back to that backup: the game reads the same thing afterwards while the files it shipped are its own again. Payloads move exactly as they are stored, so nothing is decompressed and recompressed on the way, and they are all read before anything is restored, so a failure part-way leaves the archives as they were. *Remove the overlay* unmounts the directory and deletes it, which takes every item that lived only there with it and leaves anything installed the older way where it is. Both ask first, both back up what they overwrite, and both run off the UI thread.
- **New Item Studio: Install as an overlay writes the item into a directory of its own and leaves the shipped archives alone.** Step 7 has a second install button beside the first. It builds the plan's patches and additions into a new archive directory, names that directory first in `meta/0.papgt` (where the game looks first), and writes the texture registry as before. The archives the game shipped are never opened for writing, so the backup is the mount list and the registry -- under a kilobyte -- instead of the 0.62 GB of payload files one item touches, and removing the mod is deleting the directory. A second install carries the first one's files forward, since the game takes the first directory that holds a path, and `discover_pamt_files` now orders its results by the mount list so the workbench reads the archives in the same order the game does. New and not yet confirmed in game: the button says so, and the original install route is untouched beside it.
- **Archive overlays: a mod can be its own directory instead of a rewrite of the shipped ones.** `meta/0.papgt` lists the archive directories the game mounts and the game takes the first one that holds a path, so a directory named ahead of the shipped ones overrides them. `cdmw/core/archive_overlay.py` builds such a directory from scratch -- a `0.pamt` and a `0.paz` holding only the files a mod changes, folders hashed and tiled the way the reader checks, payloads on the sixteen-byte boundary -- and `cdmw/core/papgt_format.py` parses the mount list and writes it back with the new directory first (mounting it twice updates it rather than growing the file). What the builder writes, this repository's own reader reads: an overlay written to disk lists through `parse_archive_pamt` and its entries decode through `read_archive_entry_data` to the bytes that went in. The install route that uses this is the next step; today's install still patches the shipped archives.
- **New Item Studio: a fire in the effect preview is the colour of fire.** A temperature-driven effect takes its colour from the material's temperature ramp, read at the colour curve's fourth channel, and that channel was read over a fixed 1,000. The corpus does not agree with itself about the unit: across forty shipped effects it tops out at 1.4 on one fire, 10 on an artifact aura, 268 on another and 2,039 on a third, so most curves were read at the very bottom of their ramp, which is nearly black. The fire sweep drew in (0.004, 0, 0), a red so dark it read as soot. Each curve is now normalised by its own hottest sample, so an emitter's hottest moment reads the top of its own ramp and the curve still moves the hue over the particle's life; the fire sweep lands on the ramp's (1.0, 0.12, 0.03), the same orange its sibling emitter carries as an emissive colour.
- **New Item Studio: the effect preview draws particles, and a reach that dwarfs the item keeps out of the way.** A non-looping emitter spawns inside a short window, and the simulation only spawned once its accumulator crossed a burst: a tenth-of-a-second window at ten bursts a second reaches 0.99 by the last frame inside it, so nothing ever spawned, every period, for ever. A window now fires one burst as it opens, which took the blood-smoke effect from 0 particles to a drawn plume and the fire sweep from 0 to 289. An emitter whose colour curve and emissive colour are both black multiplied its sprite to nothing, which for an additive blend draws absolutely nothing; with no colour to go on, the sprite is now drawn on its own colours. And an effect built for a boss reaches twenty metres around a one-metre sword, where its frame is a pair of columns crossing the view: above six times the item's length the frame starts hidden, with the size line saying so.
- **New Item Studio: the item a weapon effect sits on is drawn as itself.** Overlay comparison exists so a replacement can be read against the original, so the renderer skips the reference role in the solid pass and draws it as a wire ghost instead. In the effect dialog the reference *is* the subject, so the item showed only as a wire, and its materials carry no base colour out of the package builder, which the renderer draws as a black body on a dark background: between the two, the thing the effect was being placed on was invisible. A scene can now ask for `reference_draw: "solid"` (the Mesh Editor keeps the wire), and the effect package tints the item's own materials a light neutral. The helper also gained `--capture-package`, which renders a prepared package to a PNG from a form that is never shown, so what a scene draws can be checked without opening a window.
- **New Item Studio: the stats and perks page says what the game's own rows carry.** A stat the studio adds took whatever number was typed, with nothing to judge it by: the five shipped rows carrying AttackSpeedRate hold 30 to 90 million, so the 1,000 the box started at is three orders of magnitude out, and an item can behave strangely in play while its tables read fine. The Stats step now measures the shipped corpus (6,578 rows in about a fiftieth of a second), states the range and median for the stat being added, starts the value at the median, and the plan warns when an edit lands far outside it. The perk details identify the 108 of 190 gems no shipped item carries, the plan says how many chosen perks are in that position, and it warns when the socket-slot list has to grow past five, the longest any shipped row carries.
- **New Item Studio and archive patching: the two places that spent minutes in Python loops now take seconds.** The PA checksum every archive write recomputes ran at 6.1 MiB/s in Python with the GIL held: on the 15 archive payload files a single new item touches (0.62 GB) that was 1.6 minutes of a window that could not repaint, and a 174 MB file alone took 30.3 s. The native accelerator gained a `checksum-job`, so the same 0.62 GB is hashed out of process in 0.58 s (1,070 MB/s), and a build without the helper still falls back to the Python hash. The material combiner's albedo and normal synthesis walked every texel through `pixelColor`/`setPixelColor` (6.2 million calls on one imported sword); both passes are whole-image NumPy now, which takes the viewport package a model import builds from 29.2 s to 5.7 s. The array passes are byte-for-byte identical to the loops they replace, including Qt's single-precision `QColor.redF()` conversion, and the loops remain for a build without NumPy.
- **New Item Studio: the icon is cropped from the view you set up, the effect shows how far it reaches, and each step hides what it is not using.** *Take the icon from this view* captures the viewport at its own size, so what is on screen is what lands, and hands it to the Mesh Editor's own region picker (`AlignmentIconSelectionDialog`): drag the rectangle and that crop becomes the 512 x 512 icon, with the grid and the gizmo hidden for the capture and back afterwards. *Place in viewport* for a weapon effect now draws the effect's own bounding box as a thin twelve-bar frame around the anchor and says the reach against the item's own length ("14.8x the item (1.08 m)"), with **Fit the reach to the item** for a scale that starts sane and **Show the reach** to take the frame away again. The steps hide the blocks they are not using instead of greying them: the perk catalogue, the effect's rows, the item-group list, the shop picker and the import's own controls appear when they are asked for, the name and the description are labelled fields, and the two 115-character checkbox labels and the widest buttons moved their sentences into tooltips, so no step scrolls sideways at a 1280-wide window. The chosen-perks box is as tall as what it holds, two rows at the least and the eight-perk cap at the most, instead of a fixed 110 px with the buttons floating beside an empty list.
- **New Item Studio: a second item never takes the first one's identity, and the imported textures are the right way up.** The studio remembers the item key and model stem every plan hands out (kept in the settings between sessions) and reserves them for the next item, because the snapshot cannot see an item until it is installed and the archives are read again; after an install the studio re-reads the archives itself, so the next item is allocated its own key and stem instead of overwriting the one just written. A glTF, GLB, OBJ or DAE source is now built with the vertical texture flip the Builder's own **Flip V** applies (their V origin is the bottom, the game samples from the top), with a checkbox on step 3 to override it. Step 7 shows a progress bar and says what it is doing while the plan, the export, the install or an archive read runs.
- **New Item Studio: the fit is baked into the model, so the numbers start at zero and the rings turn about world axes.** The first fit (scale to the template's length, its axes, the centre) is applied to the imported mesh itself before the viewport and the build see it; the placement on top starts at offset 0, rotation 0, scale 1, so a ring drag turns the model about a world axis through its origin the way the Mesh Editor's does, and the model never shrinks to its own units (Reset is gone; Fit to the template re-bakes and zeroes the numbers). Discarding the model no longer leaves the gizmo and the old placement on the template (a plain preview forgets the placement), and the template's decode is kept per template so a re-fit or an import does not decode it again.
- **New Item Studio: the placed model builds a plan, the preview loads ahead, the effect sits on an anchor, and less text everywhere.** Apply the placement now runs the Builder's Full Import Model Replacement at the manual placement (the imported model owns the mesh, the generated textures and the material sidecar), so the plain-PBR route finds its `.pac_xml` and the plan builds (the "carries 0 .pac_xml sidecar(s)" blocker is gone); proven end to end on the wolf sword: pac, pac_xml, the `_in` sheath pair and the textures. The step-3 viewport package builds the moment a template is chosen (shown or not; the viewport itself starts when the step shows, so the item is there on arrival), a busy bar shows while a model file is read or a placement built, and the one capture left is **Capture the icon from this view** (the window route is gone; it rebuilt the package and started a second viewport for nothing). The capture itself works again after a package swap: the helper writes captures only under the output directory it was launched with, so the host asks there. The effect placement dialog hangs the gizmo on a small orange anchor at the effect's origin instead of a box the size of the effect's reach (the untextured box drew grey and hid the item); the reach stays as a number, the emitters fold under a toggle, and the camera opens on the item. Every step intro and the placement texts are a sentence, not a paragraph; the tooltips keep the detail.
- **New Item Studio: the placement gizmo tracks the model, and the step uses the width.** The model follows the gizmo as it is dragged and lands where it was let go: the package is written with the placement already in it (`PlacementScene.placement` goes in as the scene frame's transform, so the helper's placement numbers, its pivot and the model matrix start out consistent), nothing is pushed to the helper during a drag (it draws the provisional placement itself; a push mid-drag re-based it on a stale frame, which was the bouncing and the jump at the end), the finished placement is pushed once, and the host's fallback now composes the matrix the way the helper's own drag does (scale, then the rotations about x, y, z, then the offset: the static replacement pipeline's order, so the angles shown are the angles built) and moves `placement_pivot` with the model, so the gizmo sits on the model's origin instead of the scene's centre. The model is no longer highlighted as the Builder's yellow wire: it draws as itself, textured. Step 3 is two columns, the choices and numbers left and the viewport right, with the height to itself.
- **New Item Studio: a model of your own is placed over the template in the studio itself.** Import a model file on step 3 and nothing leaves the studio: `cdmw/ui/new_item/model_import.py` reads it the way the Model Library does (`load_model_import_source`: the scene import, the source's own textures bound to a preview model), the step's viewport shows it over the template (`ItemPreviewFrame.show_placement` with a `PlacementScene`: the template as the reference, your model as the editable role, the Mesh Editor's view modes Overlay / Side by side / Your model only / Template only, a Grid toggle, the gizmo in Move / Rotate / Scale, Frame the model), and a **Place the model over the template** group carries the numbers (offset, rotation, scale per axis), **Fit to the template** (scaled to the template's length, turned onto its long and middle axes, pointed the way the template's heavy end points, centred), **Reset**, and **Apply the placement**, which runs the Builder's import headlessly over the template's mesh (`build_placed_import`: a static replacement in manual mode at exactly that placement) and keeps the result for the plan. The gizmo's drags add to the placement at the drag's start (move, rotate and scale alike); moving the model again drops the build until the next apply; the rail and the Checks say so (`model_placement_not_applied` blocks the plan). The viewport's rotation convention (the helper's yaw/pitch/roll) and the static replacement pipeline's x-then-y-then-z are the same matrix re-expressed (`xyz_degrees_from_viewport_rotation`), so what the viewport shows is what the build writes (proven on the wolf gravestone sword: the rebuilt mesh's bounds equal the preview's placed bounds). The capture window takes the placement too. The Builder-hand-off plumbing the studio no longer needs is gone: `start_new_item_model_import` and the archive-browser selection dance in the shell, the `_new_item_model_sink` in the mesh patch flow, the dependency-context override.
- **New Item Studio: the Model step's preview is textured.** Step 3's viewport now shows the item the way the Model Library and the Builder show it, textures and all: the controller's `item_preview_source()` hands the view the imported model's own preview decode (`MeshImportPreviewResult.preview_model`, the textures the Builder resolved) when there is an import, else the template's mesh decoded from the archives with its textures resolved (`build_archive_preview_result` over the whole listing's path and basename maps, now kept on the snapshot as `archive_index_maps()`, since a weapon's textures sit under `character/texture/`, not beside its mesh); the bare `parse_pac` mesh only when that decode will not go. `ItemPreviewFrame.show(source, token=)` takes a `ModelPreviewData`, a `ParsedMesh`, or a callable run off the UI thread (`build_item_preview_package` goes the Model Library's package route for a model), names each source by a token so a view already showing it is left alone and a newer one supersedes a build in flight; **Capture in a window...** takes the same source. The import's dependency context now carries the whole listing behind its maps too, so the Builder resolves the template's textures as well.
- **New Item Studio: the item in the viewport on the Model step, and an import that needs no browser.** Step 3 gets **Preview: the item as it will be**: `cdmw/ui/new_item/item_preview.py` (`ItemPreviewFrame`) shows the imported model, else the template's own, in the resident viewport the Model Library uses (orbit, pan, zoom), starting the viewport only when the step is on screen and a mesh exists and rebuilding when the template or the import changes; **Capture the icon from this view** takes the frame at 512 x 512 and makes it the item's icon (thumbnail beside the button); the capture dialog stays as **Capture in a window...**. The import no longer depends on the Archive Browser: the controller builds a bounded dependency context from the studio's own listing (`import_dependency_context`: the template's family files, everything under the family's model folder, every entry whose basename starts with the family's model stem), the shell hands it to the flow (`_new_item_dependency_context`, honoured first by `archive_workflow_dependency_context` for the entries it carries), and the Builder opens over the template's mesh with the chosen file at once; the browser-selection route stays as the fallback when the studio brings no context.
- **New Item Studio: any of the game's stats on the new item, carried or not.** The Stats and prices step gets **Add a stat the row lacks**: a list of every StatusInfo entry (the ones some shipped weapon or armour ladder carries first, the rest marked "no shipped equipment carries it: unproven"), a starting value, and **Add stat column**; the column joins the table after the template's own stats, the draft's edits shift with it, and **Remove selected added column** drops it again (a template's own stat can be edited, never dropped). `NewItemDraft.extra_stat_keys`, `stat_grid_for(..., extra_status_keys)`, `controller.status_choices()`. The plan already wrote a stat a level lacked through `level_with_stat`; it now says so in the summary (`added_stats` in the manifest) and warns that a rebuilt stat list is unproven in game until the first check. The page's line says what the template's row carries, that every cell can be changed, and what was added here.

### Fixed
- **New Item Studio: repeated items and interrupted work no longer overwrite earlier output, foreign overlays, or the current draft.** Plans now carry a draft revision and are cleared by every identity, icon, model, stat, perk, shop and group edit; a plan that finishes after another edit is discarded, and an install refreshes the already-open studio's archive snapshot before another item is allocated. Existing DMM archive groups are read as mod bases and their files are carried into the next export, while CDUMM, DMM and JMM packages are built in a sibling staging directory and published only when complete, so cancellation or a metadata failure leaves the destination unchanged. Game overlays now carry a CDMW ownership marker: numeric groups belonging to another mod are never reused or removed, and install, migration and removal restore every touched file after a post-backup failure or cancellation. Mod-folder and icon-folder inspection moved into the planning worker; controller, item-preview and effect-preview shutdown is non-blocking and keeps live workers visible to the app's close sweep; model-import extraction folders are released on failure, replacement, discard and close. The Output step also keeps its longer explanations in tooltips and disables write actions until the current draft has a plan.
- **New Item Studio: effect placement presets and Fit it to the item keep the imported item on screen.** The camera was made to follow the item by replacing the editable effect role's bounds with the item's; the host also uses those bounds to move and scale the effect, so a later fit followed a fabricated box at the effect offset and could leave the real item and character outside the view. The camera now fits the stable reference role and the effect keeps its own bounds. The dialog also replaces its two introductory paragraphs with one Effect/Model row, marks the active standing view, groups the side panel as **Placement** and **Preview**, keeps mouse help in the viewport tooltip, and says **Apply** instead of **OK**.
- **New Item Studio: a failed import no longer takes the app down with it, and says what the file actually holds.** The bootstrap's progress bar is deleted when the panels replace it, and `busy_changed` goes on firing for every operation after that. A lambda was still calling `setVisible` on the deleted C++ object -- and calling it whatever the flag said, because the flag was inside the argument -- so any busy operation after the panels were built ended in `libshiboken: Internal C++ object (QProgressBar) already deleted`. A lambda has no receiver for Qt to disconnect when the widget dies, which is why it survived to be called; it is a guarded slot now. The import that triggered it was a zip holding only an FBX, and "holds no importable model (.dae, .glb, ...)" reads like the file is broken rather than the wrong kind: the message now names the file it found and says to export it as glTF, GLB, OBJ or DAE.
- **New Item Studio: Glow lists the imported model's own materials, from the moment the file is read.** It listed the template's material parts -- `cd_phm_02_hammer_sub_0002` for a model whose own materials are `lambert1`, `Inside` and `Outside` -- which are not words a reader can act on and not theirs to light in any case. The list is the materials the imported file declares, in the order it declares them, each one once; the route finds the wrapper each of them draws through, which is a mapping it already builds. They come out of the scene the importer read, so they are there when the file is chosen rather than after **Apply the placement**, and the step follows the import as well as the result.
- **New Item Studio: an imported weapon lands with its grip on the template's grip, not its middle on the template's middle.** The first placement scaled and turned the model and then matched the two bounding-box centres. Two weapons the same length can carry their mass very differently -- an axe whose head is most of it has its grip far from its middle -- so the handle landed half a weapon from where the template holds one, and every such import had to be dragged back by exactly that much. Along the long axis the fit now matches the ends away from the heavy ends, which is the same reading of the centroid it already trusts to decide which way round the model goes; the short axes are still centred, and an import whose centroid says nothing is centred as before.
- **New Item Studio: the item in the effect viewport draws with an imported model's textures too.** Whether to carry the item's textures into the package was decided by looking at `SubMesh.texture`, which is the name a `.pac` family carries. An imported model binds its own files through the preview attributes and leaves that name empty, so the check found nothing and the item was painted flat grey -- the same symptom as before the last fix, from a different cause. Every place a submesh can name a texture counts now.
- **New Item Studio: a model that glows by factor rather than by map keeps its glow.** A glTF material can be emissive with no emissive texture at all -- `emissiveFactor` is a colour -- and the Verdict axe's blue gems are exactly that (`emissiveFactor [0, 0.805, 1]`, no map, on a material with no textures of any kind). The plain-PBR route read only the maps, so those parts fell back on their dark base colour and came out black in game. The importer already keeps the factor as `_emissiveColor` and `_emissiveIntensity` preview parameters; the route reads them now and writes a solid intensity map in that colour, which is what "this whole material glows" means. The strength carries across as it stands rather than through an invented scale: across 969 shipped weapon sidecars the game's own `_emissiveIntensity` is 1.00 at the median, and 27 of the 32 that state one round to 1.
- **New Item Studio: the effect viewport's backdrop, the imported model's textures and its glow parts all waited on something that had not happened.** Three separate faults with one shape. The backdrop was sent as the presentation payload's quality colour, but the helper sets a *colour override* from the reader's remembered preference before its first frame and `_backgroundColorOverride ?? settings.BackgroundColor` means the override wins: the choice changed nothing. It travels as an override now, and a headless gate proves it at the clear colour rather than at a field (linear 0.04374 to 0.00518 for `#101014`). The textures and the glow list both waited for the Builder result, which only exists once **Apply the placement** has run: before that the item drew from the bare scene mesh, whose geometry names no textures, and the glow list was empty. Both now use the import's own decode, which is the same textured mesh the Model step draws, and its material wrappers under the template's names until the result can rename them.
- **New Item Studio: the effect dialog's panel no longer folds in on itself, says less, and draws the item with its own textures.** Asked for less height than it needs, a column of layouts does not clip: it compresses every child below its minimum, and the four placement rows ended up drawn on top of each other. The panel scrolls now, and has a width it cannot be squeezed below. The intro, the reach line and the spawn-mesh caveat were four paragraphs above the controls; they are a line each, with what they dropped in their tooltips. And the item drew as flat grey because the package carried no material resources -- a `.pac` names no textures, they live in the `.pac_xml` -- so the dialog now takes the applied import's own preview decode, which does name them, and the tint pass leaves a textured material alone.
- **New Item Studio: Glow lists the imported model's own parts, and only when there is one.** The list was the template's material wrappers by their template names (`cd_phm_02_sword_handle_0040`), including parts the import does not own, which the plain-PBR route rewrites for nobody: ticking one did nothing. It lists the parts the import owns, labelled by the source material the reader's own model gave them, with the wrapper name in the tooltip. Without an imported model the group is disabled, because the route that writes a glow runs only for one.
- **Effect previews: an effect that names a spawn mesh reads when no meshes are handed in.** `build_effect_preview`'s four optional arguments defaulted to an empty tuple, which answers a truth question the way an empty mapping does and is never asked anything else -- except `meshes`, which the one path that resolves a spawn mesh calls `.get` on. An effect whose emitter names a spawn mesh raised `AttributeError` there instead of previewing. They default to an empty mapping now.
- **New Item Studio: an effect whose emitter file the game does not ship is previewed as the effect it is, not as one puff.** An effect names its emitters by path, and a third of those files are not in the archives at all: across 160 weapon effects, all 319 of their emitters name a file that is missing. What the emitter file would have said then fell to the reader's defaults, and those were a spawn window of zero seconds and a loop count of nothing, so 267 of those 319 emitters were drawn as a single burst at t=0 and then silence. The stand-ins are now measured from the 562 emitter files the game does ship: five particles a burst, a burst every 20 to 50 ms, lives of 0.65 to 1.25 s, a spawn window of 0.2 s, and looping, which is what all 208 of the shipped emitters that state a loop count state (none states anything else). 290 of the 319 now loop. They apply only where the file is missing; an emitter the archives carry is read as before, and the note says which of the two happened.
- **New Item Studio: the effect's reach reads as a frame rather than a cage, in a colour that is not the anchor's.** The reach was twelve full bars in a dimmer shade of the anchor's orange, which left two orange things on screen and no way to tell which one was meant to be dragged. It is now eight corner brackets -- an eighth of each edge from each end, so every corner is stated twice, once along each axis meeting there -- in a cool blue that competes with neither the anchor nor the particles, which are warm by nature.
- **New Item Studio: the effect preview's particles are soft-edged sprites instead of hard-edged sheets.** A particle quad carried only its sprite's UV, and for a flipbook that is the cell's coordinate, not the quad's, so the shader had no way to know where the quad ended and faded nothing: a smoke sprite drew as a grey diamond with a knife edge across the item it was being placed on. Each corner now carries where it sits on its own quad, and the outer quarter fades out. The mask read `max(alpha, luminance)`, which overrode a real alpha channel with the sprite's grey and filled the whole cell; a texel now uses its alpha, and only a fully opaque one falls back to the sprite's strongest channel (the same rule the emissive encoder uses, because a blue glow reads as bright as a white one). The particles also sample through a clamped linear sampler of their own rather than the mesh pass's wrapping one, so one flipbook cell cannot bleed into the next.
- **New Item Studio: an imported model no longer inherits the template's glow, which was lighting the whole weapon.** A template like `cd_phm_02_sword_0040` draws with `SkinnedMeshEmissive`: cyan at intensity 10, over an intensity map cut for the template's own mesh. The plain-PBR route kept that colour and that intensity and bound whatever the importer had generated for the emissive slot, which is a flat mid-grey (BC5U, measured at exactly 0.5 across the texture). Half strength everywhere times ten is a sword lit like a torch with its albedo washed out, which is what an imported blade came out as in game. A model that brought no emissive map of its own now gets none: the binding is dropped, the wrapper goes back to `SkinnedMeshStandard`, the flat map leaves the payload with it, and the plan says so. The glow is available on purpose instead, under **Glow** on the Model step.
- **New Item Studio: a template whose prefab has an ambiguous pointee can be used again.** The prefab rewriter refuses to guess at a pointee whose trailing length field the file does not pin down, because guessing wrong corrupts the file, and that refusal stopped whole templates: `Cigar_OneHandSword`'s two sheathed prefabs each carry one, so the moment an item wanted prefabs of its own -- an imported model, or an effect -- the plan stopped with "The pointee at 0x690 has 2 possible length fields". A cloned stem is the same byte length as the one it replaces (`cd_phm_01_sword_0054_04` becomes `..._9054_04`), and a same-length swap moves nothing, so no length field is the answer to anything: those now go in place through the rewriter that relocates nothing. A swap that would change the length still refuses.
- **Archive overlays: the mount list now counts itself, which is what the game was refusing over.** The low byte of `meta/0.papgt`'s third header field is the number of directories the file holds, and the game reads that rather than counting the records. This module kept the header verbatim, so every overlay it mounted listed one directory more than the header admitted, and the game answered "There may be a problem with the game installation. Please reinstall." The one install that ever started was one where the field happened to already say 34, because the game itself had written it during an earlier session; a verified-clean game put the field back to 33 and the same overlay stopped working, which is what made the fault look like it came and went.
