Material Authority is Advanced's content rather than a collapsible inside a group inside Advanced -- its own header is gone and it reads as the section body. Parts & Routing is folded into `Part Setup`: the per-part inspector comes first, and the routing overview that used to be the whole tab (the source, original, and mapping trees with their bulk actions) follows it, so one section answers both which part and where it goes. Nothing was removed -- every tree and button is still there, in the new place -- and the deferred mapping-table build that used to start when the Parts tab was shown now starts when Part Setup is opened. `Complete source-owned mesh/material swap` stays top-level, deliberately.
- **Glow is set in Part Setup and nowhere else.** Material Authority carried its own accent-glow slider plus glow-colour and glow-strength override rows, and Part Setup carried a per-part emissive toggle, colour, and strength -- two places asking the same question for a whole model and a part respectively, and a reader could not tell which of the two the preview was showing. The three Material Authority rows are removed from the form. Part Setup's controls still take the same write path underneath, and a Builder-level test drives them and asserts that both the colour and the strength reach the part adjustment and the resident renderer's material update, so a glow is a glow and not only a colour.
- **The build now reports when its own two accounts of a texture slot disagree, instead of assuming they match.** Material resolution is described twice: once by the import transaction, from the routing it performed, and once at the package boundary, resolved against the package and the archive. They agreed because they read the same pipeline, and nothing checked it, so a slot the import believed it wrote to a path the finished sidecar never references was invisible. The two are compared before export and any difference is reported as a warning naming the path and what each side said. Only presence and resolution are compared, because the two name semantics differently and a translation table between them would be a third thing to keep true. Warnings rather than blockers deliberately: no measurement exists yet of what differences a legitimate build produces, and a rule nobody has measured should say what it saw rather than stop a build the first time it fires.
- **The build log now says what happened to each imported texture, not just where it went.** Its account of material resolution was a run of lines reading `source material, slot kind, file -> output path`, truncated at sixteen, with no indication of whether the output path would actually be written. A slot routed to a file the package never produces looked identical to one that was. Each row now carries its status and its conversion — `[generated; png->dds]`, `[copied; none]`, `[missing; unknown]` — and the block is preceded by a count per slot kind, the number of slots with no packaged file behind them, and the exact paths of any missing *required* texture. The rows are rendered from a manifest the import transaction now owns rather than assembled independently by the log, so the two cannot disagree about what a build wrote.
- **A full replacement is now blocked before commit when its sidecar names a texture the package does not carry, not only when the missing one is a visible colour.** The final package preflight blocked an unresolved base-colour or emissive binding, because a grey mesh is what a reader notices; every other role was counted into a `Missing final DDS payload path(s)` summary line and the package built anyway. For a replacement whose textures the package owns outright, that is a partial output — a normal, mask, or material binding written with no file behind it, which the game resolves against whatever the target had, or against nothing. Whether the package owns its textures is now read from the operation rather than from a flag, so this applies to Full Import Model Replacement and the complete source-owned swap and does not apply to a geometry-only replacement, where an unresolved support map correctly means the target keeps its own. The blocker names the material, parameter, and path, and like every other package-contract blocker it can be taken past with the unsafe-export acknowledgement, which is logged and confirmed again before a direct archive patch.
- **A build whose options stopped describing the operation they were built for is now refused instead of written.** The six flags that decide what a replacement replaces travel a long way between the Builder's accept and the bytes: through a preset that asserts several of them, a preview, a worker, and three modules that each re-read them to infer intent. Nothing checked that they still added up to the same operation, so a full replacement silently reduced to geometry-only, or an imported material authority silently switched back to the target's, would have produced a mod and reported success. The options carry the operation they were classified from, and the static build compares the two before it writes anything: a disagreement fails closed and names which flag contradicts which operation. Options that nobody classified — every preview construction — carry no operation and are not checked, because inventing one for them would guess at the intent this exists to verify. No route that exists today can trip it; that is pinned by a test asserting the accept path's own classification and derivation never disagree.
- **The Builder's Output review now names the operation a build will run, and what it keeps as well as what it replaces.** The strip reported counts — removed targets, sources used, DDS rows, sidecar state — and left the question a reader actually has unanswered: after this build, does the target still have its own material bindings and texture files? That answer was spread across six option flags set from clone mode, the complete-swap switch, and five checkboxes, so predicting it meant reading all six. The strip now ends with what the build replaces, and the tooltip names the operation by the same name the workflow uses — Modify Original Mesh, Replace Geometry Only, Replace Geometry and Material Bindings, Replace Full Mesh and Textures, Replace Materials and Textures Only — followed by the geometry, material-binding, and texture authority and the retained and replaced resource lists. It is answered from the classified operation rather than by re-reading the toggles, which is how a summary ends up agreeing with the controls and disagreeing with the build. A Modify Original session reads `replaces nothing` until the working mesh is actually edited, and `replaces geometry` from the first edit onward, because that is the point at which the export stops serializing the target's own geometry.
- **Solid (Textured) now textures the pane you are looking at as soon as that pane's own materials are resident, instead of waiting for every pane in the scene.** Readiness was all-or-nothing across every role the resident package carried, so an Original pane that was still compiling a full character's archive textures, or that had failed outright, held the Imported mesh grey behind it even though its own materials had already been applied — and after twenty seconds the request gave up and put the control back on Faces (No Textures). The active pane settles the request; the other pane keeps resolving in the background and textures itself when its own correlated update lands, and a failure on one pane no longer un-textures the other. Each pane now reports its own stage — not published, compiling, uploading, textured, no textures, or failed with the compiler's message — so a textured-view timeout names the stage each blocked pane stopped at rather than only which pane it was, and runtime diagnostics carry the same per-pane status.
- **A Mesh Editor command whose Edit Mesh session ended while it was running no longer applies its result.** The request id identified the newest command but said nothing about the session it was issued against, so a Finish, a Cancel, or a renderer restart between submitting a command and its result arriving left a result that was both newest and stale, and it was applied to a working state that had already moved on. Commands now record which session they belong to, and a result from a retired one is dropped with a stable `EDIT_REQUEST_STALE` code naming both generations. Anything waiting on the command to drain still completes, so a Finish does not hang behind a result that was discarded. Selection strokes and resident mutations keep the stroke, sequence, and revision correlation they already had.
- **Finish Edit Mesh can no longer report a saved edit after the resident renderer was lost, and the Edit Mesh session says which state it is in.** Whether Edit Mesh was open, closing, or already closed was inferred at each call site from whichever combination of worker timing, pending dictionaries, and status text happened to be readable, which is why Modify Original could race its own worker and stop after preparation with only a status message. The session now moves through explicit states — idle, preparing, active, applying a command, finishing, committed, cancelled, and recovery required — and every refused move is recorded rather than silently ignored. A renderer that stops while edits are uncommitted puts the session into recovery, and recovery has no path to committed at all: a finish acknowledgement arriving after the loss is refused with a reason instead of reporting the edit saved. Entering Edit Mesh now follows the helper's acknowledged scene frame rather than the click that requested it, and a finish that a gate refuses returns the session to active rather than leaving Edit Mesh disabled for the rest of the session. Runtime diagnostics carry the state, whether it holds uncommitted edits, which moves are legal from here, and the recent transition history.
- **The resident renderer now reports which owner decided whether it is sampling textures.** Texture sampling has two writers on purpose: a presentation payload that names a display mode is answered by that mode, and one that does not is answered by `use_textures_by_default`, which is how the shared preview host toggles textures at all — it leaves its stored mode on `textured` permanently and moves only the flag. The cost of the second writer was that a viewport drawing flat under a mode named `textured` looked identical whether the mode had resolved that way or a mode-less republish had overridden it, and that ambiguity is what made the two-owner regression take several passes to pin down. Both renderer status payloads now carry `texture_sampling_owner` beside `textures_enabled`, reading `display_mode`, `quality_default`, or `unset`.
- **Mesh Editor material publications are queued and correlated instead of coordinated by pending flags around a latest-wins compiler.** Which pane's materials reached the resident helper depended on resolver order, pane configuration, launch timing, and which compile happened to be running: a new request stopped the active worker with no record, and the single pending slot could remember only one waiting request and dropped the rest silently. Every publication now carries a publish id, the session, the process and package generations, and its roles, and a coordinator decides what runs, what waits, and what was displaced. Identical work for a role is folded into one queue entry, so clicking Solid (Textured) repeatedly cannot start a compile per click; newer work supersedes that role's queued work but never displaces the running compile without recording a cancellation; a compiled payload that has been sent keeps its role outstanding until the matching acknowledgement lands; and a result or acknowledgement from a replaced compile or a restarted renderer is recorded as stale rather than allowed to mark a pane ready. Runtime diagnostics gain a `publications` section naming the active, queued, awaiting-acknowledgement, and finished publications with the stage each one stopped at, so a textured-view timeout identifies the publish id rather than only the role.
- **An external model imported through Import Mesh now opens as a complete source-owned swap, and the switch for it is visible.** `Complete source-owned mesh/material swap` defaulted off and sat under *Advanced > Material Authority > Unsafe Expert Controls*, three collapsed sections down, arming itself only if a Material Authority slider or profile was touched. A first-time import that went straight to Build Mod therefore took the legacy route: the imported textures overwrote whichever original DDS files the target material referenced, with the target's grime, detail, tint, and colour-blend layers still applied on top. The checkbox now lives in a `Materials` group at the top level of the Setup tab beside Transform, and it opens checked for OBJ, DAE, glTF, GLB, and ZIP sources; a game mesh used as the source keeps whatever the In-Game Mesh Swap flow chose, and Modify Original does not show it. Unchecking it still restores the legacy route for that session.
- **Expert Material Authority controls follow the complete swap, not the unsafe-export acknowledgement.** Shader roughness/metal/shine, neutral tint RGB, preserve scratch alpha, preserve target layer response, route source colour to layer slots, mask alpha, and the expert routing values in the Color slot and PBR/mask slot combos were enabled only after `Allow unsafe material preflight export` was checked, which is itself enabled only in complete-swap mode: two levels of gate for controls the export honours regardless. They now enable with the complete swap. The acknowledgement keeps its actual meaning, pre-answering the preflight question that Build Mod and the export dialog ask anyway, and the expert warning label says which state it is in.
- **A failed final preflight now asks before a direct archive patch, the same way it does before a loose export.** The blocker dialog offered `Export Anyway (Unsafe)` for loose export but only `OK` for direct patching, and the override checkbox's tooltip said it was ignored for direct patch. The dialog now offers `Patch Anyway (Unsafe)`, the override rides the retried commit for either destination, and the log names which one it continued. The direct-patch confirmation that lists the archive files to be written, and the backup it takes first, are unchanged.
- **Build Mod no longer refuses, or greys itself out, when the exact Material Authority preview has not been confirmed; it asks.** The resolved state can sit unconfirmed for reasons the Builder cannot resolve from its own controls: a resolver that failed, a resident compile the original PAC refuses, a preview that never acknowledged. The button was disabled with the reason in a tooltip, and a click that got through was met with `Build is blocked`, so the user read about a setting they could not reach. The button now stays enabled whenever export is otherwise allowed, the tooltip still names why the state is not exact, and the click asks `Build anyway?` with the reason and the caveat that the mod is exported from the current material profile rather than the confirmed preview state, so its textures may not match the viewport exactly. `No` cancels; `Yes` takes the profile route the export already used whenever the .NET preview was not embedded. All fourteen catalogs carry the prompt.

### Fixed
- **A selected part stays highlighted in the Mesh Editor viewport instead of flickering on and then going dark on the next refresh.** The resident viewport is told what to highlight two ways: a pick sends the highlight directly, and every preview refresh republishes the whole presentation snapshot, highlights included. The snapshot's factory bound the dialog's shared highlight sets with `context.get(key) or set()`; those sets are empty when the factory binds, empty is false, and so it bound a fresh private set for each one and never saw a selection again. Read off the protocol log, each pick was `selection_update` for the part, an acknowledgement with that part highlighted, and about a hundred milliseconds later a second acknowledgement with nothing highlighted, riding the refresh. The same binding covered the hovered part, the part-adjustment map, and the UV state, so a hidden or transformed part was also being reported to the renderer as neither on every republish. The snapshot now binds the dialog's own containers, through a helper whose whole job is to not mistake emptiness for absence, and four callback factories that bound the shared texture-file list the same way, which the reader's added textures append to, take the same route. A test drives a real Builder, picks a part, and reads every presentation state sent afterwards.
- **Build Mod no longer refuses an imported weapon because its skin weights had to be matched from far off the target's surface.** The rig transfer for a static replacement measures how far each target vertex is from the source triangle it took its weights from, and when the 95th percentile exceeded 5% of the source bounds it raised `Skin-weight transfer for target N is too far from the source surface`, the preview build failed, and Build Mod was blocked. That distance is a quality warning, not an error: an imported blade never sits on the handle-and-guard surface of the PAC it replaces, so the check refused exactly the builds it exists to serve. The native core had already computed the nearest-surface weights and returned them with `transfer_distance_warning` set; the Python wrapper read the flag, raised, and threw the weights away, and the build turned that into a hard stop. The wrapper now keeps the weights and carries the flag in the transfer report, and the build appends a summary line saying the rig was matched from a distance and how far, then completes. The Rig panel's own Transfer Bone Weights takes the same path and now applies nearest-surface weights to a far source instead of falling back. The two tests that pinned the refusal now pin the warning-and-build.
- **Dragging a part upward with the gizmo no longer puts it back on the grid the moment you let go.** The placement round trip was correct end to end -- the renderer reported the new position, the Builder applied it, the offset spins showed it, and the authoritative frame carried it back -- and the mesh still landed where it started. It was not snapping back; it was being re-floored. In `grid_flat` alignment the fit measures the lowest vertex and lowers the fit offset to rest it on the grid, and it measured that from the *full* transform, manual offset included: lift the mesh by 0.11 and the lowest vertex rose by 0.11, so the fit offset dropped by 0.11 to cancel it. Read straight off the protocol log, the editable model matrix's translation row was identical across two drags while the automatic matrix moved by exactly minus the manual delta. The floor is now measured from the automatic placement, which is what it is a property of, and the manual offset lifts the mesh off it. The grid origin had the same fault from the other side -- it followed the editable bounds, so it rose with the mesh and a lift looked like no lift against the one height reference the reader has -- and now sits at the automatic floor. This is the frame builder both the resident preview and the final export share, so the built mesh lands where the preview showed it.
- **An imported model's emissive colour and intensity now survive into the Mesh Editor preview, so a gem authored as green base with a red glow renders as a red-glowing gem rather than a flat green one.** The glTF importer read `emissiveFactor` correctly, the material manifest carried it, and the resident renderer received it -- and then the Material Authority parameter update that follows every publication cleared it. That update sends `emissive_intensity: null` for any part it believes has no emissive, which the renderer takes as an explicit clear, and it believed the gem had none because the parsed-mesh-to-preview conversion copied every preview field onto the preview mesh except `preview_material_parameters` -- the only place a texture-less material's factors live. The colour had a second gap: intensity was resolved from the imported material but colour never was, so once intensity was kept the glow would have been white. Both are read now, and an imported colour sits between a part-level pick and the profile's global accent glow, because it is more specific than the latter and less than the former.
- **Selecting a part in Part Setup now selects and highlights it.** The part combo delegated to the source tree by testing whether the tree already held a row for the picked index. That tree is populated lazily in chunks and lives on a tab the reader may never open, so for any row it had not reached yet the pick fell through to the clear-selection branch: nothing highlighted, no part controls loaded. The combo now takes the same route the viewport pick already used, which materialises the row on demand, selects it, syncs the highlight sets, and pushes the selection to the embedded viewport.
- **The placement gizmo's move, rotate, and scale handles all work, and a released part stays where it was dragged.** The resident renderer reports a gizmo drag as an *absolute* placement, nested under `placement`; the host read `translation` from the top level of that payload, found nothing, and fell back to `(0, 0, 0)`, so every drag reported no movement and the part returned to where it started on release. Rotate was never routed at all: the host has rotation signals and the Builder connects handlers to both, but nothing emitted them. Scale had no signals or handlers anywhere. Every consumer of these signals adds what it receives to a base captured at drag start -- the contract is a delta -- so passing the absolute would have been right only for the first drag from the origin and wrong on every drag after. The renderer now emits an exact `begin` sample carrying the placement a drag started from, written immediately rather than coalesced behind the next update, and the host subtracts it and routes by the active tool: move, rotate, and scale each reach their own signals as deltas. In the embedded Mesh Editor, where the tab owns the controller, the same three tools already reached the transform spins as absolutes; scale is now proven there against a real Builder, and a `begin` sample is treated as an intermediate rather than published as a frame.
- **Import Mesh and Full Import Model Replacement crashed before the setup dialog could open.** A workflow preset flag was threaded through four links of the setup chain and not the fifth, so `MeshImportSetupSelection` was constructed with a keyword it had no field for and every import raised `TypeError` at the prompt, whichever entry point started it. The field exists now, and a contract test walks the whole chain -- the async entry point, the preflight dispatcher, the prompt, the selection, and the dialog -- because the gap was a name present in one signature and absent from the next, and no existing gate could see it: the Builder gate constructs the dialog rather than the prompt, and the prompt cannot run headless because it opens a modal.
- **The archive writer no longer overwrites a PAZ record's index with its checksum.** Every shipped PAMT stores its per-PAZ record as (index, checksum, size); the patcher read it as (checksum, size, reserved) and wrote the recomputed checksum over the index and the size over the checksum after every patch. The game tolerated it, which is why it went unnoticed. Both the in-place patch path and the new rebuild path now leave the index alone and write checksum and size where the shipped tables keep them.
- **Picking an edge tool in Edit Mesh no longer resets the selection shape from Lasso or Rectangle back to Brush.** `selection_mode` meant two different things: which element kind a tool operates on (vertex, edge, face, part) on an action descriptor, and how the reader is dragging (brush, lasso, rectangle) in the resident protocol. The two met in one controller field, and the tab normalised whatever arrived through a table that folds `vertex`, `edge`, and `face` onto `brush` — so Loop Cut, Edge Split, and Bridge each declared `edge`, and the reader's shape was silently replaced. The Builder's tool rail did the same thing from the other direction, publishing its element kind as the gesture on every state refresh. The resident helper never saw any of it: it accepts only brush, lasso, or rectangle and discarded the element name, so the host and the viewport also disagreed about the shape from that point on. Element kind and gesture are now separate fields end to end, including two keys in the session-state payload rather than one, and the shape table stays as the compatibility path for callers outside this split.
- **Enabling Gizmo, or any other presentation change, no longer puts the Mesh Editor camera back to its opening framing.** The camera was carried inside the replayable presentation snapshot, which is republished for a whole class of reasons that have nothing to do with the camera: a gizmo or grid toggle, a part highlight, a display mode, a preview refresh, an accepted scene frame. A replayed camera moves the viewport, and one carrying `fit_to_view` refits the zoom and zeroes the pan outright. The renderer's own "I already applied this camera generation" guard was the only thing suppressing it, and that guard does not survive the context re-initialisation a package apply performs, so the orbit and zoom the user had set were lost. A camera now rides exactly the publish that follows its own command and no later one; it stays in the retained snapshot only as the value the next camera is compared against, because dropping it there would make an identical camera look new and republish it once per accepted frame. The embedded Mesh Editor snapshot also stopped synthesising a camera: it answered with a per-mode state saved at the last mode switch, or failing that a hardcoded yaw -35 / pitch 20 carrying `fit_to_view`, neither of which is where the reader left the camera.
- **Solid (Textured) now textures an imported model in Mesh Editor instead of timing out and dropping back to Faces (No Textures).** The resident launch package deliberately carries no textures (`textures_on_demand`), so every pane is textured by a later publish. Modify Original mirrors the resolved originals onto its exact clone, and the Original pane has its lazy resolver, but an external import's own textures, bound to the working mesh at preflight, were never sent by any path: the `editable_imported` role never became ready, and every textured request on the wolf sword over `cd_phm_01_sword_0039.pac` ended in `acknowledgement_timeout` even once the Original pane's materials had compiled and applied. This was masked while the Original compile itself failed on the shared-texture and mask blockers fixed below. An imported model now publishes its own materials when its working model joins the resident session, which is the boundary that owns them; the Original publish that follows is deferred behind that compile and flushed after the acknowledgement. Driving the Imported publish from the Original resolver, as the first version of this fix did, left the Imported pane textured only as a side effect of resolving a different pane, so a single-pane workflow skipped it and an Original resolver error took a valid Imported pane down with it. Runtime diagnostics gain `pending_imported_material_publish` alongside the other pending material publishes.
- **Solid (Textured) no longer fails on a PAC whose material slots share one mesh-declared texture, in Mesh Editor and Edit Mesh.** On `cd_phm_01_sword_0039.pac` the `Acc_0038` and `Acc_0037` slots both reference `cd_phm_02_acc_0037.dds` through the mesh itself rather than a material XML. The native archive core keys such `embedded_mesh_reference` bindings by material-name stem and attributes each to the slot whose name matched, so `Acc_0038` received the row with the neighbouring owner and the PAC material graph counted it as a cross-owner binding: `binding_conservation` failed, the compiler refused the whole Original pane, and every pick of Solid (Textured) rolled back to Faces (No Textures) with `Could not compile resident PAC materials for the Original pane`. A mesh-declared reference whose file is one of the submesh's own resolved channels is now that submesh's own binding, whichever slot the native core named; the resolver's content-hash prefix on the extracted file is matched by basename. Bindings a material XML declares keep the strict cross-owner rule, and the reproducer pins both directions.
- **Solid (Textured) now survives Material Authority resolving a base texture for an imported model in Mesh Editor.** The resolver hands the resident material path the Builder's replacement preview model, whose submesh rows are the slotted `ModelPreviewMesh`; the snapshot that stages its DDS bindings wrote the parser-level `texture` attribute onto them, which that class has no slot for, and the `AttributeError` unwound the resolver's completion callback as an unhandled exception. The textured request then never completed, the Mesh view control fell back to `Faces (No Textures)`, and Material Authority stayed at `Resolving exact preview…`, which is what left Build Mod refusing. The snapshot now writes only the attributes a row can carry; the base binding lives in `preview_texture_path`, which the compiler reads ahead of `texture` anyway, and the parser's `SubMesh` rows keep receiving both.
- **Solid (Textured) no longer fails on a PAC whose layer selector mask is also its packed material texture, in Mesh Editor and Edit Mesh.** A raw normal, height, or material `.dds` the resident viewport packages verbatim is deliberately not decoded for synthesis, and that skip is relabelled so it does not read as a hard failure. On `cd_phm_01_sword_0039.pac` the guard's and blade's `_colorBlendingMaskTexture` is the same `_m` / `_ma` file the material channel packages, and it is also the grime selector both the layered-normal and material-layer compositors read through `preview_texture_path`; skipped, it produced `normal mask unreadable:` and `material layer mask unreadable:grime:r`, neither of which the relabelling covers, and the compiler refused the whole graph, so every pick of Solid (Textured) rolled back to Faces (No Textures) with `Could not compile resident PAC materials for the Original pane`. Any input the combiner reads as a selector mask is now decoded even when it is also the raw channel; the raw `.dds` is still packaged for the viewport, and a mask that genuinely fails to decode still blocks the compile. The decode diagnostics record the count as `raw_channel_mask_decoded_count` beside the existing deferral count.
- **The .NET renderer reported a viewport rectangle it had stopped rendering to, for the rest of the session.** `renderer_status` publishes the D3D surface's client size, but the payload is cached behind a key built from the outer `MeshViewport` control's `Width` and `Height`. The surface is a child of that control and settles to its final size after the parent does, so the two disagree for a while, and nothing else in the key moves when only the surface resizes. The first status built during that window was therefore kept and served indefinitely: measured on the real-game gate, the renderer published `1047x1195` for a pane its own `ActivePaneBounds` and Win32 `GetWindowRect` both reported as `1242x1195`, leaving the status the outlier of the three. Anything trusting it to aim at the pane, or to size a capture or a hit test, was being told the pre-settle rectangle. The cache key now includes the surface size through a new `MeshViewport.RenderSurfaceClientSize`, which reuses the existing `PaneSurfaceSize()` rather than introducing a second idea of what the pane is. On the gate the reported viewport now follows the surface to `1242x1195` and the harness's `status_disagreed_with_settled_pane` flag goes false, with every other reconciliation field unchanged.

- **PAC vertices can carry eight bone influences and only six were being read.** The record's six 10-bit palette slots at bytes 20-27 are not the whole skin: two further influences are indexed at bytes 12-15 as halves holding whole numbers, weighted by bytes 34-35, and live whenever the low six bits of byte 39 are not 63. The game's own vertex shaders index the same bone matrix buffer with all eight, so a vertex using seven or eight was being reported with two influences missing and with the remaining six renormalised as though they were the entire row. On one guard-class body that is 7,023 of 9,502 vertices; on a helmet, 310 of 519. Meshes that never open the gate, which is most of them, are unaffected and parse exactly as before. The weights are now normalised by their own total rather than by 255, because the stored bytes are not normalised across all eight: a six-influence row sums to 255 and an eight-influence row to about 500, and the shader divides the accumulated transform by the accumulated weight, so only the proportions are meaningful. The exact topology serializer is deliberately unchanged: bytes 12-15 and 34-35 are protected by its ownership mask, so a derived vertex still inherits them unchanged from parents that already had to agree on them, and the six-slot capacity blocker still refuses anything it cannot author exactly. The generic replacement writer likewise still authors only the palette lanes and now says so, reducing a wider row to its six strongest rather than appearing to write all of it.
- **Every PAC character mesh was being read with the wrong normals, and re-authoring one corrupted its tangents.** The 40-byte record's normal word is not three packed components. The game's own shipped vertex shaders store only x and y, in bits 10-19 and 20-29, reconstruct z on the unit sphere, and take its sign from bit 30; bits 0-9 hold the tangent's y component and bit 31 the bitangent handedness. The parser read bits 0-9 as the normal's z, so it returned vectors that were not unit length at all: measured across five body and head PACs, the normals it produced had a median length of 1.1336 and only 8.5% were within 1% of unit, which biased everything downstream that lights, exports, smooths, or refits a PAC mesh. They are now unit length for 100% of those vertices and match the shader's own decode exactly. The writer had the mirrored fault: it packed the normal's z into bits 0-9, silently overwriting part of the tangent frame every time a normal was authored, so an imported or rebuilt mesh could light incorrectly under its normal map while its geometry looked right. It now carries bits 0-9 and bit 31 across untouched and authors bit 30 from the normal's own z sign, which the previous code left at whatever the donor record happened to hold, inverting z on any import whose normal disagreed with its donor. The exact topology serializer is unaffected: it admits a derived vertex only when every parent agrees on the protected mask, so all parents share a z sign and a blend between them keeps it, and a regression pins that rather than leaving it as an argument. The layout was recovered read-only from the shipped `.padxil` shader cache rather than inferred, and `tools/pac_shader_consumer_study.py` reproduces the evidence.
- **Edit Mesh selection no longer remains blue without committing, disappears after deformation tools, or leaves Select unable to work again.** The resident update queue treated a selection result at the same revision as an in-flight Move/Grab/sculpt frame as an uncorrelated supplement, rewrote it to the geometry request ID, and could also merge two independently correlated requests into one envelope. The helper correctly rejected that mismatched authority, but the host still reported success, leaving the provisional overlay and stroke state stranded. Positive request IDs are now strict ordering barriers: selection waits behind the earlier frame, keeps its own ID, and a failed enqueue or cancelled request sends a correlated rollback instead of false success. Lost mouse-up, focus, and tool-change paths share one idempotent gesture closer that retires every paint/lasso/cache field, so a later Select starts cleanly. Move, Grab, Smooth, Inflate, and Pinch preserve the committed selection, while topology adopts the native remapped selection rather than clearing the Builder mirror. The expanded hidden production-path gate held a geometry acknowledgement open while selecting all 20,000 fixture faces, then immediately completed a second independent Select; both correlated in 41.4/44.3 ms, the worst Qt heartbeat gap was 18.1 ms, and every deformation tool retained the exact pre-stroke selection.
- **Edit Mesh Grab, Move, Smooth, Inflate, Pinch, and immediate Subdivide now keep the exact result shown at release.** Grab's local preview held the initial brush footprint, but native code recomputed weights under the moving cursor, so a far drag could draw a spike and then save little or nothing; native Grab now captures its initial weights and center once. Sculpt updates were reduced to the newest endpoint at both coalescing layers, and an absorbed mouse-up sample was then forced to zero strength, making Smooth settle backward and Inflate/Pinch weak or intermittent. Coalesced sculpt packets now retain the complete compact pointer polyline, native code integrates exposure along every segment, the visible-depth mask covers that full path instead of only its final brush circle, the final moving sample remains active, and Smooth/Inflate/Pinch display the resident native stream instead of a different local approximation. Every deformation end publishes one cumulative correlated geometry frame, commits one resident-history unit, and uses a lightweight Builder undo marker instead of taking as many as three complete mesh snapshots after mouse-up. A topology click made while Brush/Lasso selection is still provisional now waits for terminal selection authority and discards the stale helper snapshot; selection failure or cancellation rejects that queued action, so Subdivide cannot silently target the preceding selection. Dense stroke previews switch to binary geometry above 256 selected vertex equivalents: on a 40,000-vertex Move packet the native round trip fell from 161.42 ms to 45.30 ms (71.9% faster), while tiny updates remain inline. The expanded hidden production-path gate drove 320 updates through each of Move, Grab, Smooth, Inflate, and Pinch with 311-317 updates coalesced per tool, zero release snap-back, 16.7-19.0 ms terminal reconciliation, and a drained dispatcher for every tool.
- **Large Face and Vertex Brush selections no longer freeze Edit Mesh for seconds when the mouse is released.** The terminal selection was treated as a geometry edit even though no vertex moved: the Builder could snapshot the complete working mesh twice and the base mesh once, rebuild five derived reports, then serialize the same large selection through three competing publications. Selection-only commits now bypass geometry history and synchronous reports, refresh visible Parts counts from the cached workspace, publish no full selection during begin/update and one correlated authority at release, and omit its duplicate and unchanged geometry-layer state from the following session status. The worker-produced selection state is reused through the complete terminal path and direct selection commands, so the Qt thread performs no selection-prune session or geometry-layer read. UV-island source-face membership is cached with multiplicity so UV Map remains correct after topology fan-out, and localized UV/Rig panels are resolved by identity; Rig reuses its cached part summary. A hidden 10,201-vertex/20,000-face production-path run with UV Map active selected all 20,000 faces with zero Qt-thread session or geometry-layer reads, zero Builder snapshots, zero duplicate embedded applies, and zero nonterminal selection updates; the terminal handler measured 5.6 ms, mouse-up-to-dispatcher-idle 34.6 ms, and the worst bracketing Qt heartbeat gap 15.6 ms.

## [0.11.0-alpha.5] - 2026-08-12

### Fixed
- **Edit Mesh Lasso now commits the polygon that was drawn, and dense Face/Vertex Brush selection stays responsive instead of freezing and catching up.** Mouse-up was omitted from a fast lasso and a second release-only simplifier changed longer outlines; visible-depth selection then bounded its depth raster to the lasso's nearly coincident start/end points instead of the polygon, producing the sparse selection left after release. Mouse-up is now an exact polygon point, drawn/provisional/native paths are identical, and the depth raster uses the full lasso bounds. Dense Face Brush now queries 16-pixel screen buckets instead of every triangle, stores a face spanning more than 16 cells only once, updates retained wire and growing face/vertex GPU overlays incrementally, and acknowledges intermediate native snapshots without replacing the newer local echo; the complete authoritative selection still publishes at release. On the same hidden production D3D11 one-million-vertex Face Brush workload, input-to-present p95 fell from 59.977 ms to 9.245 ms (84.6% lower), the worst WinForms heartbeat fell from 62.560 ms to 23.762 ms (62.0% lower), and throughput rose from 32.9 to 123.5 FPS (3.8x). The million-vertex Vertex Brush profile passed at 87.8 FPS with 11.682 ms p95 and a 17.449 ms worst heartbeat; final 180,000-vertex Face/Vertex/Lasso profiles passed at 175.0/174.9/175.0 FPS with 5.912/5.911/5.924 ms p95 and exact terminal lasso polygons.
- **Archive Browser now builds the first textured `.pac` preview in one pass and keeps its established material choices while doing 68.2% less native package work.** The initial request discarded the saved **Load textures** preference, built an untextured package, then queued the same PAC again for textures; even a complete bounded dependency snapshot was subsequently ignored while the native core rebuilt the full PAMT index. The first request now carries the saved texture preference, and a complete prepared snapshot becomes the native in-memory index without reopening the archive catalogue. The preview provider also recovers path tokens followed by a prefab length byte, includes the identity skeleton, and resolves the same `_sp`/`_n` base siblings and embedded material-name DDS files as the full native index, so the optimized PHW package retains the exact prior head, hand, and body base textures and the same 26 asset-family rows. On three cold `cd_phw_00_nude_00_4001.pac` runs, median native package generation fell from 828.591 ms to 263.277 ms (68.2% faster), before counting the removed second package job; the complete headless selection pipeline after archive open measured 510.357 ms on a fresh prepared cache.
- **PHW skin no longer turns uniformly shiny when a damage response has no selector texture.** Archive Browser's native material combiner skips a layer-scoped damage response when its required mask is absent, but the Mesh Editor package combiner treated that absence as 100% body coverage and replaced the varying skin roughness with one flat value. Mesh Editor and Edit Mesh now follow the same fail-closed selector rule for missing or unreadable masks, preserving the base per-pixel roughness instead of applying the damage response everywhere.
- **Solid (Textured) now stays selected for PHW models whose visible textures are stored as direct mesh references, in both Mesh Editor and Edit Mesh.** The native archive loader had already resolved these DDS files exactly, but the resident material registry treated its `embedded_mesh_reference` label as an unsupported PAC shader parameter; material compilation therefore failed and both selectors correctly rolled back to `Faces (No Textures)`. The registry now accepts that label only with the native `embedded_mesh` + `mesh` provenance pair and routes the declared semantic without weakening unknown-parameter failures. The packaged-app gate now opens the escaped `cd_phw_00_nude_00_4001.pac` case instead of the easier PDW sample, and still requires renderer-confirmed texture resources and draws in normal and Edit Mesh modes while hash-checking the source archives.
- **Solid (Textured) now returns after switching to another Mesh view mode, including inside Edit Mesh.** The helper's own mode selector updated the active renderer fields but left its saved pane contexts behind, so the next host publication could restore `Faces (No Textures)` even though the control said Textured. Both panes now change atomically. Native DDS preview conversion also uses short staging names before moving results into the content-addressed cache; long real-game texture names had exceeded the Windows path limit and the native helper's per-file failure was discarded, leaving a valid PAC permanently untextured. Cache hits now revalidate every packaged resource, failed native items remain in the decoder diagnostics, and a read-only packaged-app gate opens the exact archive PAC, switches No Textures to Textured in normal and Edit Mesh modes, waits for all queued scene/material/presentation work, then requires renderer-confirmed texture sampling, live shader resources, and textured draw calls while proving the PAMT and PAZ hashes are unchanged. The packaged gate now uses a genuine shown D3D11 window placed off-screen: its former `SW_HIDE` launch suppressed every swap-chain paint and tested a state the GUI never enters. Failed gates write their diagnostic path and close cleanly instead of showing an unhandled-exception dialog, while readiness-watchdog records distinguish an expired current deadline from a queued stale callback before any helper restart.
- **Edit Mesh selection, X-Ray, and the complete hidden tool gate now agree with the native session instead of silently skipping work.** Select All previously derived its target parts from an empty incoming selection, while Invert omitted every completely unselected part; both operations now visit every editable native submesh. Clearing the X-Ray checkbox now returns to Vertices instead of testing an unreachable display-mode index. The composed headless gate semantically exercises selection operations and shapes, all six axis nudges, history, topology, parts, geometry layers, Colour, Morph & Refit, all seven display modes, and camera controls; its reference run covered all 9 rail rows and classified all 75 discovered controls, wrote all 3,293 correlated events with zero drops, and reconciled 320-update Move and Grab streams with zero snap-back and worst submissions of 11.42 ms and 11.56 ms.
- **Archive Browser's `.pac` Modify Original preflight is 74.8-79.7% faster, and completed loads no longer leave a delayed `Preparing Alignment` flash behind.** The preflight worker copied its already-parsed mesh through two native snapshot/restore round trips and ran generic submesh routing immediately before replacing it with the exact original-clone mapping. It now makes two independent in-memory worker copies and goes directly to exact routing: on `cd_pdw_00_nude_00_0001.pac`, cold/warm preflight fell from 418.816/306.052 ms to 85.060/77.136 ms, while the clone substage fell from roughly 219 ms to 0.307/0.266 ms. Embedded construction also resets the force-show timer that `QProgressDialog` arms in its constructor instead of merely leaving the dialog hidden. A new read-only real-PAC gate runs cold, refresh, and warm transitions, captures Archive Browser and the fully revealed Mesh Editor, rejects any progress-dialog or partial-child show event and any surviving force-show timer, and verifies the source PAMT and PAZ hashes remain unchanged.
- **Archive Browser no longer reveals a half-constructed Mesh Editor while a `.pac` static-replacement session is opening.** Import Mesh and Modify Original keep Archive Browser active through preflight, mount and show the embedded builder under its still-hidden parent, close `Preparing Alignment`, and only then queue one Mesh Editor activation. The real offscreen construction smoke now verifies that mount, progress close, show, and reveal occur in that order for both entry paths and records an exact skipped-reveal reason if construction, parenting, visibility, or progress state is wrong.
- **Solid (Textured) no longer fails when Modify Original starts the original-texture resolver, and its failure diagnostics now retain the resolver stage and traceback.** The composed dialog factory omitted the native package-path setter, material-manifest publisher, and descriptor converter that its late texture callback reads, producing an `AttributeError` before any texture could be resolved. Those dependencies are now installed with the rest of the callback state, resolver transitions are kept as a bounded session history, and Mesh Editor diagnostics report the correlated request, worker/thread state, failure stage, and full traceback.
- **Edit Mesh interaction logging no longer freezes selection and sculpt gestures by writing every protocol event to disk on the Qt event thread.** A bounded background flight recorder now batches the same correlated session/request/revision/tool events, exposes queue depth, drops, write failures, and a recent-event tail in diagnostics, and records outbound, queued, coalesced, completed, cancelled, failed, and stale decisions across all tools. A new entirely hidden synthetic gate drives the production D3D11 `Solid (Textured)` renderer, Brush/Lasso/Rectangle selection for vertices, wires, and faces, a 320-update held face-selection stroke, Move, Grab, Smooth, Inflate, Pinch, Topology, Colour, and Morph & Refit through the composed Python/C++/.NET path; the reference run wrote 1,268 events with zero drops, drained to queue depth zero, and reconciled every live tool result without snap-back.
- **Skin no longer looks broadly dirty or mottled in Mesh Editor and Edit Mesh after layered normal synthesis.** The material compiler was deferring the native-selected `_00` body normal, substituting an alternate `_01` material-wrapper normal, and interpreting the empty blue channel in BC5 normals as negative tangent Z. It now anchors synthesis to the selected macro normal, excludes unrelated wrapper alternatives, and reconstructs positive Z from XY before applying authored detail. On the exact `cd_pgm_00_nude_00_0001.pac` body, synthesized tangent tilt mean/90th percentile fell from 0.468/0.774 to 0.088/0.169; hidden front and back Vortice captures retain the fine authored skin grain without the broad dark pattern.
- **Mesh Editor's cold textured-material preparation is now another 64.5% faster without reducing texture resolution or changing the generated maps.** The PAC material compositor now performs its per-pixel roughness, specular, occlusion, legacy-response, and layered-normal arithmetic in bounded NumPy row chunks, while manifest path rebasing resolves its two stable roots once instead of repeating filesystem canonicalization throughout the material graph. On the same cold `cd_pgm_00_nude_00_0001.pac` flow used for the prior fix, the median fell from 5.103 seconds to 1.812 seconds (98.1% faster than the original 93.214-second path); all 13 generated support-map images remained pixel-identical to the scalar implementation.
- **Mesh Editor and Edit Mesh now preload the current Archive Browser texture package, keep skin matte, resume without helper restarts, and reveal one settled layout.** Modify Original acquires a live lease on the already-decoded native package and queues its texture state as the embedded editor opens, so `Solid (Textured)` no longer needs a second selection; automatic Archive Browser texture refreshes defer while the Builder owns the asset instead of competing with it. Resident activation now sends the helper's live material signature, forced sync requests bypass local deduplication, and a bounded generation-correlated material-sync deadline replaces the ordinary activation watchdog while the helper is waiting, removing the `.NET/Vortice Preview is resuming` restart loop without allowing a failed sync to hang forever. Native material ownership is resolved to the actual submesh before serialization, explicit skin evidence prevents packed blue-channel coverage from reclassifying skin as metal, and detail/damage/grime height maps remain layer inputs rather than global relief, eliminating the wet highlights and cross-part white marbling seen on `cd_pgm_00_nude_00_0001.pac`. Qt also stops running publication retries for authoritative DDS files it cannot decode, and already-resident height DDS maps are not synthesized and discarded: the exact PAC's cold textured-material stage fell from 93.214 seconds to 5.103 seconds (94.5% faster). Authoring panels and their permanent rail sections are realized while the child window is hidden, the child is sized before reveal, and Edit Mesh no longer expands placement flanks just to replace them; its real-helper entry median fell from 3.208 seconds to 1.153 seconds (64.1% faster).
- **Solid (Textured) in Mesh Editor and Edit Mesh now means textures are actually decoded and bound, rather than merely requested or listed in a manifest.** Resident package loads reject declared texture sets when none can be decoded, material acknowledgements carry renderer-confirmed texture readiness, and the shared D3D path verifies each declared base map has a live shader-resource binding before the mode can settle. The native preview cache can no longer prune DDS files referenced by the result it has just returned or by another live preview package, and direct base, normal, material, and height bindings reach the viewport before slower PAC skin/material synthesis upgrades the same resident material in the background. Every accepted geometry-package swap invalidates the previous package's material acknowledgement before presentation replay, fails any staged export update, fences already-sent texture and parameter mutations by package generation, then automatically republishes current materials before restoring Textured. While loading or after any failure, both visible selectors and presentation replay honestly show `Faces (No Textures)` and the selection remains a real retry. The regression launches the real hidden D3D form with a generated four-colour texture and requires decoded resources plus a live material binding; a missing-texture package must fail before publication, and a decoded-but-unbindable package must roll back with the prior document, scene, geometry buffers, material bindings, texture SRVs, display mode, and resident-scene count still usable.

## [0.11.0-alpha.4] - 2026-08-11

### Added
- **Preview Settings now sets the resident viewport's wireframe and vertex-marker colours, beside the background and grid.** The untextured solid renders blue-grey and the topology overlay drew close enough to it that a solid-plus-wire display melted together. The colours already existed, but only behind the Edit Mesh colour buttons, so a reader who was previewing rather than editing could not reach them. `d3d11_wire_color` and `d3d11_vertex_color` sit on the same Gizmo tab, persist with the other viewport colours, and ride the presentation-quality payload that already carries the grid colour, so no new helper protocol capability was needed. Defaults reproduce the renderer's own `MeshOverlayColors.Default` exactly, only a changed colour is pushed through (the host republishes after every accepted frame), and a colour picked inside Edit Mesh still outranks Preview Settings for the rest of the session, the way an in-viewport background choice already does.
- **Edit Mesh now has persistent geometry layers and a mesh-internal selection clipboard.** Copy captures complete selected faces with their UVs, normals, tangents, skin weights, material routing, source maps, and native extra attributes; Paste creates one undoable `Selection copy N` layer while preserving separate material/source fragments internally. The new LAYERS panel supports rename, visibility, ordering, deletion, and active-layer isolation. Base mesh is permanent, hidden copies remain saved but are excluded from output, geometry and layer generations autosave atomically after 750 ms, and Modify Original can resume compatible drafts for the exact source-asset SHA-256 without loading or deleting incompatible work.
- **Edit Mesh selection can target Vertices, Wires, or Faces, with separate customizable committed and live colors.** Click, Brush, Rectangle, and Lasso honor the chosen target; the overlay preference format moves to v3 while retaining v1/v2 wire and vertex choices, and the selected colors now reach the D3D11, GDI, and WPF render paths.
- **Morph & Refit now has per-garment fitting controls for cloth and hard parts.** After binding, select one or more garment parts and apply an Enabled toggle, 0-200% follow intensity, Surface or Rigid mode, and 0-5% clearance measured against the driver's bounding-box diagonal. Surface is the existing flexible barycentric behavior; Rigid transports each vertex in its bound triangle's orthonormal local frame so armour and other hard pieces do not inherit triangle stretch. Clearance runs after either mode and pushes a vertex outward when the moving body would penetrate it, including at 0% follow. Each application is one undoable resident edit, different bound garments can keep different settings, Reset and Bake rebase them, and existing bindings default to Enabled, Surface, 100%, and zero clearance.

### Changed
- **Fresh and resumed Edit Mesh sessions now open in Orbit, and whole-part viewport picking is disabled.** Runtime camera, tool, element selection, and Undo history are intentionally not restored from drafts; Parts & Routing/PARTS is the only whole-part selection surface, while element selection works without a part filter inside the active geometry layer. Switching tool pages inside the same live session still preserves the active tool.
- **Selection strokes now use one correlated, latest-wins background transaction instead of per-dab native work and history.** Each gesture keeps at most one active and one pending update, carries all unsent swept coverage, retains newer provisional geometry across stale acknowledgements, and creates one history entry only when its final response arrives. Lasso completes provisionally on mouse-up, topology waits for the gesture to drain, and a failed worker restores the pre-stroke selection instead of leaving partial history.
- **Subdivide/Refine now applies the shared 200,000-faces-per-submesh limit throughout the resident path and operates on the complete selected region.** Faces subdivide directly; Wires and Vertices expand to incident faces and remap back to split wires or original/generated vertices. Predicted overflow is rejected before mutation with the submesh and counts, while native Undo/Redo restores geometry and selection together in one step.
- **The main workspace strip now follows the kind of work being done instead of mixing full editors into asset and utility groups.** The visible order is Assets, Mesh Editor, Placement & Animations, Texture Upscaling & Editing, and Tools. Assets keeps Archive Browser, Model Library, and Icon Creator; the texture group spells out Texture Workflow, Texture Replacer, Texture Recolor, and Texture Editor; and Tools now holds Retrofit/Repackage, Format Explorer, Translations, Research, and Text Search. Mesh Editor and Placement remain direct workspaces without an extra nested tab bar, saved and detachable-tool navigation still targets the same tool keys, and Format Explorer's location paths use the new visible labels.
- **Edit Mesh binds dense Morph & Refit garments without scanning every body triangle for every vertex.** The resident native core now uses an exact deterministic bounding-volume hierarchy while preserving the same closest-triangle and tie-breaking result. On the synthetic 100 x 100 regression mesh, binding tests 211,065 triangle candidates instead of an exhaustive 196,020,000 (928.7x fewer) and took 0.018 seconds instead of 2.21 seconds; the candidate and driver-triangle counts are carried in the native refit report so the acceleration is guarded without a timing-dependent test.

### Fixed
- **Edit Mesh now opens its resident editing controls instead of leaving only the two preview panes visible.** The shared preview host and Mesh Editor tab were assigning scene request IDs and generations from separate counters, so a placement refresh could make the helper reject the following `mesh_edit` transition as stale. Shared authoring transforms and package-load restoration now use the tab's single latest-wins scene lane; preview-only sessions keep their existing independent scene state.
- **Archive Browser's extension picker now has its complete list the first time it opens after startup.** The first bounded archive page marked the browser ready before the separate extension-facet response arrived, which enabled **Select** while its data was still empty and made the dialog substitute a zero-count `.dds` placeholder. **Select** now remains unavailable until the current extension counts are published, then enables from that queued facet delivery without delaying the first archive page.
- **Opening Mesh Replacement Builder with a very large mesh no longer starves interface updates while its background preflight measures bounds.** The million-vertex scan now yields at the cancellation checkpoint it already visits every 4,096 vertices, so progress, cancellation, and repaint work can run while the worker continues instead of waiting behind one long pure-Python loop.
- **External-model material audits and direct-DDS preview packaging now keep declared evidence authoritative.** Embedded FBX texture references outrank unbound same-role companion filenames, and an explicit material subtype (including specular and glossiness) cannot be reclassified as occlusion merely because a temporary directory contains `ao`, so audit rows, generated roughness maps, and fallback notes are deterministic.
- **Preview texture preparation no longer leaves its source image locked on Windows.** The resize and legacy PBR-split paths now decode images from in-memory byte buffers, and the shared material combiner explicitly closes its Qt file device after decoding, so Python 3.14 can clean up or replace each source immediately after packaging.
- **Solid (Textured) loads its textures in Mesh Editor and in the Viewport section's Preview mode, instead of drawing flat until the mode is swapped back and forth.** The display mode reaches the resident helper down two channels: the narrow `viewport_display_update` the Mesh view control sends, and the presentation snapshot, which is republished after every accepted scene frame. That snapshot carries the whole Preview Settings quality payload beside the mode, including `use_textures_by_default`, which is "load textures automatically after geometry" and is off by default. The helper applied the mode first and the quality payload second, so every republish turned textures back off under a mode whose entire meaning is that they are on. Picking the mode again re-textured the scene until the next frame landed, which is what made it look random. A payload that names a display mode now lets that mode own the textures; `use_textures_by_default` stays the authority only for a payload that names no mode, which is the archive preview's own toggle. Both directions are gated, because dropping the flag outright would have stranded that toggle with nothing to catch it.
- **X-Ray draws the wireframe and vertex colours chosen in Preview Settings instead of always overriding them.** X-Ray draws topology through the surface, where the default black wire is unreadable, so the renderer substituted a fixed high-contrast palette. That substitution was unconditional, which made a colour chosen in Preview Settings look ignored the moment the display mode became X-Ray. The automatic palette is now the fallback for an untouched preference only, and the decision lives in `MeshOverlayColors` alone: the D3D11 viewport held its own fixed copy of it, and the resource metrics reported the constant rather than the colour actually drawn, so the evidence agreed with itself while the viewport drew something else. The GDI and WPF paths already routed through the shared helper and follow automatically. The headless renderer proof asserts both directions, because a fix that simply dropped the fallback would leave an untouched preference drawing black on dark.
- **A background action refused because another task is still running now leaves an error line in the live log, and in the archive log when the action was showing archive progress.** The shell's utility runner reported that refusal through the status field alone. The status field holds one transient line and the logs hold the record, so a refused action produced no trace anywhere a reader could go back to: the log simply ended at whatever the previous step had written. That is exactly how the Modify Original draft-check chain below looked like it had stopped for no reason, with the archive log ending at `Checking Modify Original drafts for <asset>...` and nothing after it. The line names the action that was dropped, so a dropped step is distinguishable from a queued one, which the deferring variant of the same runner already reports as `Waiting for the current background task to finish before starting the next step.` The archive-log decision follows the caller's `show_archive_progress` argument, because the refusal returns before the runner takes ownership of archive progress.
- **A freshly opened Mesh Editor is camera-only, instead of selecting whole parts or grabbing a gizmo handle on the first left click.** The Part Pick toolbar checkbox was removed but its widget was left checked and merely hidden, so every presentation snapshot still reported `part_pick_enabled: true` and MeshViewport kept routing a plain left click into `BeginSelectionDrag(..., "source")` outside `mesh_edit` mode. The placement gizmo defaulted on beside it, and while it is visible a click on a handle begins a placement drag through `TryBeginPlacementGizmoDrag`. Both now open off: a left drag orbits, Parts & Routing is the whole-part selection surface, and element selection stays inside Edit Mesh. Neither control was removed, so Part Pick and the visible Gizmo checkbox still turn their behavior back on, and the Gizmo checkbox now persists under `ui/mesh_alignment/gizmo_visible` exactly as the Grid checkbox already did, so off is what an unset preference reads as rather than a state forced on every open.
- **Archive Browser's Modify Original reaches Edit Mesh again instead of stopping at the draft check.** The new draft-fingerprint pass runs as its own background task and started workspace preparation from that task's completion handler, but Qt delivers a worker's `completed` signal before the thread's `finished` signal, so the shell still saw a task in flight and refused the second stage as a concurrent action. That refusal only reached the status field, so the archive log ended at `Checking Modify Original drafts for <asset>...` with no error line and nothing opened. The chained stage now waits for the shell to go idle, the way every other two-stage archive action does. Resuming a saved draft hid the fault, because that dialog's nested event loop let the first thread finish before the second stage started.
- **Wire + Vertices no longer draws solid fill, and opening Morph & Refit no longer reloads the viewport.** Preview-mode translation has one owner, Morph reuses the native submesh counts it already computed, and page activation limits redraw suppression to the tool column so source parsing, geometry upload, device/surface identity, helper PID/HWND, camera state, and presentation generation stay unchanged.
- **Long Brush and Lasso selections no longer disappear behind delayed resident updates.** Target-specific provisional overlays remain visible until their correlated authoritative response, stale replies cannot clear a newer tail, and completion converges without the old per-dab queue and repeated history churn.
- **Edit Mesh and static replacement now preserve exact PAC skin rows for proven OBJ round-trips.** When the matching `mesh_roundtrip_manifest_v2` sidecar supplies an authoritative source-vertex map, rebuild copies each original influence row instead of estimating weights by surface proximity. Skinned PAC OBJ imports without that sidecar now stop with recovery guidance, and PAC output is rejected if a rebuild changes vertex-record bytes outside the declared position, normal, or UV0 edits.

### Docs
- **`docs/architecture.md` draws the system instead of only describing it.** Seven diagrams cover the process topology and the three single-route authorities, the allowed and rejected import edges, the facade-and-owner split that most of the document's sections are instances of, the resident preview session's states with its two failure lanes, the runtime cache tree, the archive mutation pipeline, and the latest-wins worker contract. The preview lifecycle gained a section of its own, because it was previously spread across a package-map bullet and two paragraphs under Services.
- **The README names the workspaces the app actually shows.** The main strip has been Assets, Mesh Editor, Placement & Animations, Texture Upscaling & Editing, and Tools since the navigation was reorganized, but the feature table still read `Image Editor`, `Recolor Variants`, and `Placement & Animation Studio`. A diagram of the strip and its groups sits above the table, the Mesh Editor row names the selection, geometry-layer, and morph-refit work added in this release, and the helper diagram now lists `cdmw_archive_accelerator` and marks `cd_hkx` as the Rust project it is rather than a fifth C++ one.

## [0.11.0-alpha.3] - 2026-08-03

### Added
- **The resident viewport's grid is customizable: color, spacing, and line count, beside the background color.** The grid turned out to be CPU-built line lists with per-draw colors rather than anything shader-compiled, so the pipe was three presentation-quality fields away: `d3d11_grid_color` (minor lines; major lines draw the same colour lightened by the fixed palette's own +35 step), `d3d11_grid_spacing_scale` (0.1-10x on the scene's spacing), and `d3d11_grid_line_count` (4-40 lines each side of the origin). All three sit in Preview Settings on the same tab as the viewport background, persist with it, and apply live through render tuning; the defaults reproduce the fixed grid exactly.
- **Finish Edit Mesh can no longer be stranded.** Three independent guarantees now stand behind the one button whose failure locks the reader inside Edit Mesh. In the helper, a pending Finish resumes from whichever message clears its last blocker — command results included, where before only a fresh Morph & Refit state update could resume it, which quiet sessions never send — and a four-second fallback timer forces the save outright if the message it waits for was lost, trading at worst an uncommitted procedural morph value for an exit that always happens. On the host, a Finish refused because the action worker or a live stroke was mid-flight retries itself the moment that work drains, instead of silently requiring the reader to click the button again — which is exactly how "Finish does nothing" reports happen.
- **The resident viewport's background color is a Preview Settings choice now.** The renderer has accepted an `#RRGGBB` background through its presentation-quality payload all along; nothing ever sent one. `ModelPreviewRenderSettings` carries `d3d11_background_color` (defaulting to the renderer's own dark grey), the Gizmo tab of Preview Settings offers it as a "Viewport background" color button next to the gizmo colors it shares a panel with, and a change applies live through the same render-tuning lane as the lighting sliders.
- **Selecting is instant now: Brush, Rectangle, and Lasso edit mesh vertices while PARTS remains the explicit whole-part control.** Brush projects each editable part once per drag, bins its vertices into screen cells, and updates the vertex overlay on every pointer move while native depth resolution runs at the bounded protocol cadence. There is no dense-mesh cutoff, and Toggle records a crossed vertex once per gesture so painting back over it cannot silently undo the first pass.
- **Edit Mesh has brush and lasso selection, not just the rectangle.** The Selection combo has offered "Brush Select" — as its first entry and its default — for as long as it has existed, and the resident editor ignored it: every Select drag was a rectangle. The combo's three modes are real now. Brush paints the selection under the cursor as you drag, Blender's Select Circle in spirit: each throttled dab is an add (or subtract, when the operation says so) `screen_brush` selection that the native core resolves with full camera and depth awareness, so the swept path unions into one selection without holes — coalescing that kept only the latest cursor sample would have left gaps, which is why intermediate dabs apply inline off the action-worker path and only the drag's final dab records the selection-history unit. Replace starts a new selection on the first dab, the brush ring shows the paint radius (the Radius control drives it), and a plain click keeps the precise 14-pixel click pick rather than stamping a full-radius dab. Lasso sends the actual swept polygon — the native core has resolved lasso polygons all along; nothing ever sent one — with the rectangle endpoints kept beside it as the fallback older cores read. Rectangle stays exactly as it was, and part-pick drags in placement keep rectangle semantics whatever the combo says.
- **A session can now be watched from four sides at once, including the pixels, so "it flickers" becomes a timestamped list of what blinked and what happened either side of it.** Nothing here had ever recorded an interaction, so a report that the preview reloads arrived with no way to reconstruct the sequence that produced it, and every investigation started by guessing at the click that came first. `scripts/monitor_mesh_editor_session.py --all` now launches the app from source, records what you did, captures the window, and writes one correlated timeline. The capture is the part that could not be inferred: it grabs the window with `PrintWindow(PW_RENDERFULLCONTENT)`, which was measured against the real Mesh Editor viewport and does return the D3D11 swap-chain content, works while the window sits behind other windows, and photographs nothing but the app — so a recording never sweeps up whatever else is on screen. Blinks are found by a rule that separates the two things a log cannot: a region that changed and *came back* within a few frames is flicker, while content that changed and stayed is the interface working, and only the first is reported. Capture holds itself to a quarter of wall clock and drops its own frame rate to stay there, because `PrintWindow` drives the application's UI thread and a monitor that slows the thing it measures is measuring itself; a 1220x800 window costs 23ms a frame and the tool prints the rate it actually achieved. The in-app half is a Qt event filter recording clicks, focus, and which widgets repainted, hid and showed — widgets hiding is called out on its own, because a pane that hides collapses the splitter beside it and resizes the embedded viewport. It is inert unless `CDMW_SESSION_RECORDER` is set, keystroke text is redacted unless `CDMW_SESSION_RECORDER_KEYS=1` is passed as a separate deliberate act, and its trail is written to a subdirectory rather than the crash reports folder, because `latest_diagnostic_report_files()` globs that folder into the shareable diagnostic bundle and a record of every click does not belong in a file attached to a bug report without thinking about it.
- **The Mesh Editor's viewport can now be a node inside Qt's own scene graph, rather than a Win32 child window embedded in it.** The 3D view has always been a separate window parented into a Qt widget, and that seam is where the editor's positional defects came from: it stayed parented to a window Qt had destroyed on a monitor move, learned about resizes by polling and then waited for them to stop, and needed an elaborate hidden-window reveal to avoid being watched mid-assembly. A child window is also always topmost inside its own rectangle, so no tooltip, popover or overlay could ever be drawn across the model. `native/cdmw_qt_rhi` is a working `QQuickRhiItem` that renders through Qt's D3D11 RHI as an ordinary scene-graph node, with the interface composited above it in the same frame. It is a proof of concept — it draws a test triangle, and porting the mesh renderer onto it has not started — but it establishes the whole path end to end. It is C++ rather than Python because PySide6 renders through `QQuickRhiItem` perfectly well and then never lets the process exit, which is worse than the flicker it would replace. `scripts/build_qt_rhi_plugin.ps1` builds it in one command; `scripts/vendor_qt_sdk.py` fetches the exact Qt that PySide6 ships, verifying every archive against the SHA-256 Qt publishes beside it, because the plugin uses `QRhi` from Qt's private module and Qt gives no compatibility promise across versions there. The build fails outright if the two ever disagree, since a mismatch does not announce itself: it corrupts somewhere else later.
- **The Item Finder has a Stats section, and it says which slot an item equips to.** Finding a piece of armour by name and then learning anything about it was not possible: the detail pane listed evidence, names, materials, models and icons, but nothing the game itself would call a stat. `EquipTypeInfo` is now decoded — 113 rows, each a hash key and a slot name, all 113 distinct — and an item row is matched against it. The match is safe to trust because every one of those keys is above 2^24: a value equal to one is that key, not an ordinary small integer that happened to collide. 2,973 of the catalogue's 3,624 entries name exactly one slot, and the mapping is what you would expect — boots to `Foot`, gloves to `Hand`, cloth armour to `Upperbody`, and Abyssal Dragon Armor to `DragonArmor`. A row that names two slots reports none, because a guess is indistinguishable from a fact once it reaches the pane. The 651 entries with no slot at all are consumables and quest items, and the section says so rather than sitting empty. The slot also joins the search text, so typing `helm` finds every helm; descriptions deliberately do not, because folding prose into the index would make one item's flavour text match half the catalogue. `EquipTypeInfo` is the first table in `cdmw/core/table_catalog.py` marked `runtime_usable`, and only for the two fields actually proven against the corpus. A Description section sits beside Stats and shows the text recovered earlier in this release, which until now reached the resident catalogue but never the dialog that displays items.
- **Every item in the Item Finder now carries its in-game description.** Each `iteminfo` row holds a second localization key beside its display-name key, in the same shape and one id later, and nothing in the app had ever read it — all 6,508 rows carry one. Exact row boundaries made it free to pick up: 3,574 catalogue entries now have the description text the game shows, and it travels with the row through the resident catalogue and the native indexer alike.
