- **Select All, Invert and Clear no longer flash the Parts list through the change.** The selection was applied one part at a time, and each of those repaints the list, so the cost was one repaint per part on a mesh that might have dozens. The sweep now runs under the same begin/end pair the list is already repopulated under.
- **The Mesh Editor now follows the window while you drag its edge, instead of snapping into place a fifth of a second after you stop.** The editor is a native child of the preview pane, and it learned about a resize only by polling the pane's size from its own timer, then deliberately waiting for 200ms of stability before acting so that dragging would not reallocate the renderer's buffers on every step. The effect was that the pane grew immediately and the editor kept its old size for the whole drag, leaving a band of bare background down the side. Moving the window is cheap and only the buffer reallocation was worth deferring, so the resize is now pushed straight through in the same frame as the pane's own, and the wait it used to sit behind has nothing stale left to catch up to.
- **The Mesh Editor's tool flanks compose in one frame instead of painting a window at a time, so touching a row, a button or a list no longer flickers.** Every WinForms container, button and list is a separate window that paints itself, and `DoubleBuffered` only buffers a control's own painting into its own window: it does nothing about the children. Any layout change moved, resized, showed or hid those windows individually, each painting as it went with the panel's background showing through the gaps, which is what the flicker was. Both flanks and the session bar now carry `WS_EX_COMPOSITED`, so Windows composes each subtree off screen and presents it once. It is deliberately scoped to the chrome rather than set on the form: the viewport presents through a DXGI flip-model swap chain, and flip model is not compatible with a composited ancestor, so putting it on the form is what would have cost the renderer its presentation path. Nothing in either flank is an ancestor of the viewport.
- **The placeholder triangle no longer appears when the Mesh Editor starts.** The resident helper stays warm between meshes, so when the preview was made visible it was usually already holding something: the procedural scene it was warmed on, or the mesh opened before this one. Becoming visible activated that first and only then noticed the applied package was not the one selected, so the window was revealed specifically to show one model and spent the length of a package load showing another. The reveal now belongs to the load, which activates once the right package has landed. The activation contract and the three reveal sites gated on `--prewarm` were all working; this was a fourth path that reached the same window without consulting them, because it checked only that *a* package was applied rather than which one.
- **Edit Mesh no longer re-applies the whole preview presentation on every scene frame, so selecting a part or painting a stroke stops flashing a different preview mode.** Presentation state is replayable desired state, but the host republished the entire snapshot after every accepted scene frame, and a frame is published per brush pointer sample and after every selection change. Driving twelve frames with nothing changed produced twelve publishes, eleven of them byte-identical to the one before; each made the helper re-apply its display mode, overlays and role view, then rewrite the preview-mode selection, the role-view buttons, the controls hint and the status line. Because the snapshot is rebuilt from live Qt widgets, the display mode it carried could also be the pre-change value while the helper had already switched locally, which is the mode that flashed before the view settled. A publish whose content the helper is already holding is now skipped, and the paths that actually reset the helper (a package apply, a new process, a rejected apply) clear that record instead of forcing a publish past it. Twelve frames now cost one publish.
- **A momentary failure to read the Edit Mesh checkbox can no longer reveal the placement panels behind the tool dock.** Both scene modes are read out of live builder widgets at publish time, and a read that raised answered with the literal `placement`. That value goes out as an authoritative scene frame, and on the helper's side a `placement` frame while the reader is inside Edit Mesh is a genuine transition, not a redundant one: it returns the shared sections to the placement flanks, un-collapses both splitters and reveals the placement panels, and the next correct frame puts the rail back. The guard added in 53e51498 could not catch this, because it only skips a frame that changes nothing. A failed read now retains the last authoritative mode, which is what "I could not read it" actually means, and records the failure rather than swallowing it.
- **Opening an Edit Mesh tool paints once instead of in four stages.** Clicking a row flipped every page's visibility, re-laid out the list table around the moved body cell, sometimes scrolled the column, and then ran the splitter pass, with nothing holding the window still in between. Every other layout switch in the editor already held a redraw batch; the one on the click path did not. Resizing the window inside Edit Mesh also left the strip of flank the dock is resized across holding whatever was painted there last, because the splitter pass finds no width change on a resize and returns before reaching its own invalidate; the resize now asks for that region back.
- **Dragging the app to a second monitor no longer leaves the Edit Mesh builder panel behind.** The helper is a Win32 child of the Qt host widget's native window and was told that window's handle once, on its command line. Qt destroys and recreates that window on a move to a screen at a different scale, so the helper stayed parented to a window that was no longer the one on screen, and nothing in the protocol could correct it. The host now listens for the handle change and asks the helper to re-parent itself, which keeps the resident scene and the edit session rather than relaunching for them. The helper's DPI-change handler also defers its layout pass off the DPI message, because that pass holds a redraw batch and `WM_SETREDRAW(FALSE)` clears `WS_VISIBLE` for as long as it is held, which a handle recreation landing inside it would inherit.
- **A second mesh opens in a Mesh Editor that is already showing one.** Opening one mesh and then choosing Modify Original on another left the pane on the first mesh forever; only shutting the Mesh Editor down entirely and reopening it worked. The resident helper is claimed by the edit session that opens it, so a second live session cannot steal the process out from under the first. Nothing ever released that claim. Closing a mesh drops its package, its leases and its viewport but deliberately keeps the helper warm for the next one, so the claim outlived the session that made it: the warm process stayed bound to a session that no longer existed, every later bind was refused, and the next mesh's package was rejected with "Close the current editor before opening another mesh" -- about an editor that was already closed. Killing the helper was the only thing that cleared it, which is why a full restart was the only cure. Releasing the resident scene now releases the session with it, and the next mesh is handed the warm helper rather than refused. The protection is intact: a session that has not let go is still not stealable, and a suspend and resume by the same mesh costs no rebind. The helper takes the handoff over a new `authoring_session_handoff_v1` message, and a helper too old to advertise it is replaced instead of reused, because one that cannot be told its owner left would refuse everything the arriving session said and show a mesh that answers nothing.
- **A PAC vertex is read with all six of its skin influences, and an FBX export now carries the rig.** The format was documented last release but the decoder was left alone: it read bytes 20-23 as four u8 palette slots when they are two u32 holding three 10-bit slots each, followed by six u8 weights. Both ends were wrong in a way that hid itself. Reading, 3,672 of one body's slots landed outside its own 206-entry palette and its secondary bones sat a median 0.532 from the vertices they drive, against 0.088 for the packed reading. Writing, the encoder padded the unused lanes with `0xFF`, which under the true layout is not a sentinel at all but live bits of the second and third slots. Across the extracted corpus every record now decodes to weights that descend and sum to 255, every slot lands inside its palette, and the influence spread on the reference body is 2145/2869/2851/2284/1830/1761 across counts one through six — the four-influence reading could not see the last two. Exporting FBX threw the whole thing away regardless: the file held `Model` nodes and nothing else, no `Deformer`, `Cluster` or `Skin`, because the per-submesh payload carried only vertices, faces, normals and UVs, and the bone list it did send was a hierarchy with no per-vertex binding. The native writer now emits a Skin deformer per submesh and a Cluster per driving bone, with per-vertex indexes and weights and a `Transform`/`TransformLink` pair taken from the bone's bind pose, so the mesh arrives at rest rather than pre-deformed. Verified in headless Blender 5.1: 448 bones import, all three parts are bound by an armature modifier, every vertex belongs to a group naming a real bone, weights sum to 1.0 within 5.4e-08, and rotating `Bip01 R UpperArm` 45 degrees moves 1,159 vertices sitting a mean 0.26 from that bone against 0.85 for the vertices that stay put. Slots are per-mesh palette tokens rather than bone indices, so binding needs the palette resolved against the skeleton; a rigidly bound prop records its bone nowhere in the file, resolves no palette, and is now exported as geometry plus armature with no binding instead of being bound to a guess. The replacement path is unaffected: an influence row it did not change is left byte-for-byte alone, which holds for all 399,984 records in the corpus. Two smaller things fall out of the same reading. Exported bones were sized by measuring between a bone's local offset and its parent's, which are not in the same frame; a bone's length is the offset's own magnitude, so most bones drew at the wrong size in Blender. And the Placement Studio's rig preview took a vertex's second bone from byte 24 of the record, which is really the low eight bits of the fourth influence's slot — the second influence's index straddles bytes 21 and 22 and was never readable as a byte. It now takes the decoded pair, which gives 76% of a body's vertices a genuine two-bone blend where the byte reading found far fewer.
- **An exported FBX arrives at its real size instead of a hundredth of it.** A character opened in Blender came in 1.8 centimetres tall rather than 1.8 metres, and every distance in the scene was out by the same factor. `UnitScaleFactor` states how many centimetres one file unit is and an importer divides by it — Blender computes its global scale as `UnitScaleFactor / 100` — so declaring 1 told every reader the geometry was in centimetres when a game unit is a metre. The export now declares 100, alongside the `OriginalUnitScaleFactor` that Blender writes beside it, and a body whose source extents are 0.76 x 1.56 x 0.31 imports at exactly those numbers with the armature at scale 1. Nothing about the geometry moved: the vertex values written to the file are unchanged, only the unit they are declared in. Re-importing into the Mesh Editor is unaffected either way, because the importer reads the coordinates as they are and asks ufbx for no unit conversion — the same file imports to an identical report before and after.
- **Edit Mesh stops re-laying itself out on every scene frame, which is what made it flicker.** Clicking a tool or a part flashed the panels, sometimes showed a different display mode on the way through, and occasionally dropped the grid; resizing the window could briefly reveal the placement side panel behind the dock. One cause behind all of it. The helper re-asserts its interaction-mode controls whenever a scene frame is accepted, and that pass is not small: it walks two section lists setting visibility, collapses and uncollapses both splitters, lays out each split, re-applies the dock width and re-activates the presentation view. Its guard only skipped the work when *both* sides were already in placement, so the entire time mesh edit was active the pass ran on every accepted frame — and a frame is published per pointer sample of a brush stroke and after every selection change. The guard is now symmetric: the re-assert runs on a real transition and does nothing when the two sides already agree, which is what the placement half of it already said.
- **Textured skin in the Mesh Editor no longer looks wet.** The same character rendered glossier there than in the Archive Browser preview it is meant to match. Not the shader family, which was the obvious suspect and is innocent: both routes classify these parts as `skin` and get the same roughness floor of 0.30. The divergence was one scalar neither material declares. The archive route always states `roughness` 0.5 and `metalness` 0.0; the Mesh Editor route stated them only when the asset carried an explicit override or a glTF factor, and a Crimson PAC skin material carries neither. With no scalar the renderer keeps its own `MaterialRoughness` constant of 0.45, so the surface came out 0.05 smoother than the reference — enough to read as wet on skin, which is the family that shows it most. Both routes now take those defaults from one place, so the Mesh Editor asks for the same surface the Archive Browser does. Skin keeps its depth: the family floor and the +0.06 bias are untouched, and a material that does declare a value still wins over the default.
- **A preview mode chosen inside Edit Mesh now sticks.** Picking Solid (Textured) worked or did not depending on nothing the reader could see, and often started working only after leaving and re-entering the editor. Three things fought over one value. The builder's presentation snapshot hard-coded `wire_vertices` whenever mesh edit was active, and that snapshot is republished after every accepted scene frame — so a pick survived exactly until the next frame happened to land. The helper only told the host about a pick on the branch where textures still had to be resolved, so in the common case, with textures already resident, the host never learned the mode it was about to overwrite. And entering Edit Mesh forced Wire + Vertices unconditionally, discarding whatever was chosen before the door. Edit Mesh now keeps its own remembered display mode beside the placement one: `wire_vertices` is the default it opens on rather than a value it re-asserts, every pick is announced to the host, and the entry default is applied without announcing so it cannot overwrite a remembered mode before that mode is published. Leaving Edit Mesh still restores the placement view, which is a separate slot.
- **Finish Edit Mesh leaves Edit Mesh, even when the repaint behind it fails.** The button did nothing, and did nothing again on every later click. The exit unticks the builder's checkbox and syncs the session first, then repaints: pushes resident presentation state, rebuilds the textured preview model, and refreshes the mesh-edit controls. Those last three could raise, and the exception escaped into the caller, which read it as "finalization failed", reported an error, and re-published `interaction_mode="mesh_edit"` to a helper whose builder had already left mesh edit. Host and helper then disagreed about the mode permanently, and every subsequent click repeated the same sequence with the same result. The repaint is now a tail that reports each failing step through the mesh-edit event log instead of raising, so it cannot take the transition back, and the failure path no longer re-arms a mode the builder has left. A rejected scene update also names its reason in the viewport's own status line — `stale_scene_generation`, `stale_session`, `invalid_scene_frame` — because from inside the viewport the rejection was previously silent, which is what made the button look dead rather than refused.
- **The Mesh Editor no longer flashes the placeholder test triangle when it starts.** The helper is started early on a procedural one-triangle package so that the process, JIT and D3D11 device are warm before the click, and it is meant to serve that placeholder entirely off screen. `ActivateResidentViewport` reveals whatever scene is resident, though, and the Mesh Editor's resident-reuse fast path decided that the helper "already has this scene" from the tab's own cached package, a cache that survives the editor being stopped and outlives the dialog that filled it. Paired with a freshly prewarmed helper, the tab therefore activated a placeholder: the prewarm package's own status file recorded the warm-up triangle presented at 1180x638 in the pane, by a process that had never been given a real model. Reuse is now decided by the controller that owns the process rather than by that cache, and the helper refuses to be revealed at all until a real package has been applied to it, so the invariant no longer rests on host-side sequencing spread across its callers. Releasing a resident scene also falls through to the rebuild it implies instead of stopping at "Mesh .NET editor experiment is already running", which previously left that path unable to open the editor at all.
- **The item index reads the game's string tables through the reader that owns them, and stops losing 55,350 lines.** It had a second `.paloc` reader of its own: a byte scan that accepted a key only if it was 6 to 20 ASCII digits. Every non-numeric key failed that test, which is all 20,224 `questdialog` entries, 17,757 `aidialogstringinfogroup`, 15,689 `node`, 1,055 `textdialog` and 65 `quest` — 132,171 of the shipped table's 187,521 strings survived. Item names and descriptions are keyed numerically, so the Item Finder itself never saw the gap; two readers for one format is the defect, not the symptom. `cdmw/core/paloc_format.py` already decodes and re-encodes the format byte-for-byte and backs Translation Studio, and the count in the table's own footer lets it verify the walk landed where the file says it should, which a scan cannot do. Loading the English table also drops from 1.31s to 0.26s.
- **The Item Finder was missing a third of the game's items, and now finds all of them.** Item records were located by scanning `iteminfo.pabgb` for a fourteen-byte marker, and that marker is not a record header — it is a fragment of the sub-record that carries an item's display-name key, so a row whose preceding bytes differed was simply invisible. Measured against the shipped table it found 4,142 of 6,508 rows: precision was perfect, but 2,366 items could not be searched for at all. Records are no longer found by scanning. `iteminfo.pabgh`, which ships beside it and which the app described as an index/hash table, is the row directory: a row count followed by one `{primary key, byte offset}` pair per row, which gives every record an exact start and end. The repository already had a reader for it — `parse_pabgh_table`, which the Attachment tools have been slicing item rows with all along — so this routes the item index through that owner rather than adding a fourth copy of a scanner. Recall is now the row count by construction, and a `real_game` test asserts exactly that, because it is the invariant that would have caught this the first time. On the shipped archive the Item Finder's catalogue goes from 2,974 to 3,634 entries; the native indexer that produces it in the running app goes from 3,141 to 3,808 linked items in the same 7 seconds, so this costs no time. The one catalogue row that disappears is not a loss: "Pavis Leather Boots" is still indexed, absorbed into a two-variant group by the sibling row that was previously missing.
- **`iteminfo.pabgh` reads correctly for every shipped table, not just the two row shapes the editor could rewrite.** The parser recognised a two-byte count with five- or eight-byte rows and guessed between them for anything else, which silently mis-parsed the two tables that use composite primary keys. Both the count field and the key are variable width, so both are now resolved by search — a 1-, 2-, or 4-byte count, then a 1-, 2-, 4-, 8-, or 12-byte key — and the choice is confirmed against the payload, because every row repeats its own key inline. That inline check is what decides single-row tables, which fit several widths arithmetically and are otherwise ambiguous. All 134 `.pabgb`/`.pabgh` pairs in package `0008` now resolve, covering 283,076 rows: `characterappearanceindexinfo` needs the eight-byte key and `aieventtableinfo` the twelve-byte one, and a reader allowing only 1/2/4 drops both. The structured-sidecar editor's write path is unchanged and still rewrites only the five- and eight-byte flavours.
- **Picking Move or a brush in Edit Mesh sticks, and dragging with it actually edits the mesh.** Two things believed they owned the armed tool. The editor's tool rail set it and told nobody; the host kept its own copy in a hidden control that has read "Select Vertices" since it was built, and republished that copy on every refresh of the mesh-edit controls — which a vertex selection triggers. So the rail armed Move, the next selection put Select back, and because the viewport decides on mouse-down what a left-drag means, the drag that followed resolved a selection instead of opening a stroke: no stroke was ever sent, and nothing moved. That is the same defect twice, and it is one authority now. Choosing a tool on the rail announces it, the host adopts it, and the value the host publishes distinguishes Move from Grab, which it had been folding onto one entry. The host asserting a tool the viewport already has is also no longer treated as a change — it ran the rail's page sync, which is why the Topology, Colour and Morph & Refit pages closed on their own every time anything refreshed.
- **Subdivide, Duplicate Selection, Refine Smooth and Delete Selection on Edit Mesh's Topology page now change the model you are editing.** They always reached the mesh service and always mutated the edit session; what never happened was the rest of it. A command raised inside the editor took a path that pushed a new preview payload and stopped there, skipping every step the same command takes from the builder's own buttons: storing the result as the working mesh the preview and Build Mod read, bumping the edit revision, updating the vertex and face totals, recording an undo step, and giving a duplicated part the source row that makes it routable. So the geometry changed where nobody could see it, and a duplicate never became a part you could map. Both routes run the same commit sequence now, once per edit, whichever one carries it back.
- **Edit Mesh's Colour page works from a vertex or face selection instead of going dead.** It read only the whole-part selection, so selecting geometry with the Select tool left every control greyed out under "Select at least one part to recolour" — with no hint that a part selection was what it wanted. It now targets the parts the selection sits on. Colour is stored per part, so that edit is wider than what is highlighted and the page says so rather than letting it surprise you; when the selection is faces, a Split Selection Into Part button moves them into a part of their own so the colour applies to just them.
- **Choosing "Solid (Textured)" or "Solid + Wire" in Edit Mesh's Preview Mode now loads textures even when picked early.** The texture request needs a resident session to address; picked before one existed, the request was dropped on the floor — the combo snapped back to the untextured fallback and nothing ever re-sent it, so the first textured pick did nothing until the user happened to pick another textured mode later, which is why "click Solid + Wire and then it suddenly works" was the workaround. The wish is remembered now and replayed the moment a session is observed. This is also why Faces + Wire and Solid + Wire looked identical: without textures, Solid + Wire deliberately falls back to the untextured wire view rather than dropping the wire overlay, so the two modes only differ once textures actually load.
- **The placement Gizmo now appears in the Overlay preview mode.** Overlay renders as a single pane whose role is "comparison", and the pane builder only allowed the gizmo on a pane whose role was exactly "editable" — so switching to Overlay silently dropped the move/rotate/scale handles from the one view whose replacement they exist to move. Only the locked reference pane hides the gizmo now; the hit-testing, pivot, and camera paths were already consistent for Overlay, so drawing it is all it took.
- **The Mesh Editor no longer flashes the placeholder test triangle while starting.** With a real model requested and a handshake gate still down, the preview session activated the prewarmed helper's procedural warm-up scene as a fallback and replaced it moments later — a flash of a triangle nobody asked to see. Every handshake gate re-runs the launch finisher when it arrives, so the session now simply waits with the watchdog armed and the first thing presented is the requested model.
- **Sixty-three interface strings that stayed English under every non-English language are translated now, among them the Retrofit/Repackage scan summary.** The localization manifest generator recognises a helper function as producing interface text when its return value reaches a UI sink directly, but not when it arrived through an f-string — `title, text = helper()` followed by `setText(f"{title}: {text}")` hid the helper, so none of its strings were ever collected into the catalogs and the rendered line could not be translated at runtime. The generator now follows names interpolated into f-string sink arguments. That surfaced 63 uncollected strings across Retrofit/Repackage, Placement Studio's orientation hints, the attachment placement patch notes, research snapshot statuses, archive family badges, and the Help dialog's Start Here section; all 13 non-English catalogs carry translations, with technical tokens registered as deliberately source-identical.
- **Edit Mesh no longer throws you back to Selection > Select after every action.** Using Move, a brush, or Clear selection re-armed Select and the tool-properties column jumped back to the Selection page. The rail reveals its pages through one method that also asserts the page's default tool — correct when a reader clicks a rail button, wrong when the same method runs to restore the rail's appearance during a layout re-activation, which happens on every redundant `mesh_edit` frame. Because `RailPageOwnsTool` recognises no page for orbit, morph or topo, the re-assert almost always resolved to Select. Revealing and choosing are now separate: a rail click still selects the tool it names, a re-reveal changes nothing about what is armed.
- **Build Mod is no longer blocked by the unsafe material preflight.** Routing non-cloth geometry into a cloth/flag runtime draw slot is a real in-game risk, but it is the modder's call to take. On a complete source-owned swap the preflight promoted that finding to `report.errors`, and `StaticReplacementReport.ok` is simply `not errors`, so the build refused outright — passable only through a checkbox most people never found. The finding is now always a warning: it still names the exact source-to-target routing and what it can do in game, and the export proceeds.
- **Selecting a part now highlights it in the preview again, from Parts & routing and from Mesh Editing.** The predicate that answers "is a geometry tab in front" already recognised both, but the d3d11 package-lifecycle factory never exported it, so the three callback families that read it out of the context — highlight sync, source-tree selection, and transform drag — each got `None`, fell back to False, and no selection ever produced a highlight id. Exporting it restores all three; the highlight rule itself is unchanged.
- **Model Library searches when you submit, not while you type.** Two defects met here. In the Local Library view every character scheduled a fresh filter pass over the whole scanned set, which made a long query unusable. Worse, every row request read the edit box live, so even with that removed a sort, a texture filter, or a Hide downloaded toggle would silently apply a query that had never been submitted. Typing now only records the draft and labels it; Enter, Search, Clear, and a view switch are what promote it to the applied query that rows are actually filtered by.

On a complete source-owned swap the preflight promoted that finding to `report.errors`, and `StaticReplacementReport.ok` is simply `not errors`, so the build refused outright — passable only through a checkbox most people never found. In the Local Library view every character scheduled a fresh filter pass over the whole scanned set, which made a long query unusable. - **Every column layout was written to disk one section at a time, and an interface-language change paid for all of them.** `make_tree_columns_persistent` saved the whole layout and called `QSettings.sync()` — a synchronous disk write — from `sectionResized` and `sectionMoved`. Both fire far more often than a layout actually settles: once per pointer sample while a column divider is dragged, across all thirteen trees that use it, and once per header a translated label re-laid out. A single switch to German flushed fifteen times and spent 224–431 ms of its 600 ms doing it, all on the UI thread. The write is debounced onto the settled layout now and the flush is coalesced, so a burst costs one of each; a drag that ends and a window that closes both still persist, because the close path flushes what is pending. The Archive Browser's own header persistence already debounced through `schedule_settings_save` and is unchanged.
- **Changing the interface language walked every page in the window, including the ones nobody had opened.** The translation pass visited all 4,453 widgets of a fully realized shell — 4,345 of them on closed tool tabs and collapsed settings sections — probing ten possible text properties on each, for 46,357 property reads. None of it could be seen: a hidden widget is not drawn, and Qt raises a Show event on every widget of a subtree as it is revealed, which the localizer's application-wide event filter already turns into a deferred pass. That is the same route a lazily built tool tab has always taken. The walk now prunes at each hidden subtree and translates it when it is opened, so a tab the reader never opens is never walked at all. The root is deliberately never pruned, because a top-level window reports itself hidden until it is first shown and the startup pass for a saved non-English locale runs before that. Measured offscreen on the fully realized shell, blocking cost with the flush fix above: German 600 ms to 56 ms, Japanese 791 ms to 62 ms, back to English 648 ms to 53 ms, with follow-on work of 40 ms to 18 ms and property reads down from 46,357 to 1,570.
- **Changing the theme walked every responsive control on the window twice.** A full theme apply queued `_apply_responsive_control_minimums` once from inside the UI-font step batch and again after the theme steps that follow it. The later pass is the one worth keeping — it sees the fonts every intervening step installed — so the earlier one is gone.
- **A blocked geometry Undo threw away the state it was meant to restore.** While an active Mesh Editor session owns geometry history, the Python restore path refuses to run and says so. Undo popped its snapshot, called that path, released the snapshot in a `finally` regardless, and then wrote "Undid ..." over the refusal — so the one recoverable state was destroyed by an operation that changed nothing and reported success. A refused restore now puts the snapshot back and leaves the explanation on screen. Reset Geometry had the same shape and is fixed with it.
- **Full Reset left the morph sliders disagreeing with the mesh.** It zeroed the post-edit deltas the way Scope Reset does but skipped the re-apply that follows, so the sliders kept reading whatever had been dialled in while the working mesh was back at base — and the next nudge of a slider jumped the geometry to catch up.
- **Selecting a part destroyed a finished rebuild report.** Every session-state refresh cleared it, and part selection goes through that path, so a report could no longer be inspected, previewed or packaged after a click that changed no geometry. Staleness is now decided by the session's geometry revision, which only a geometry command moves.
- **An ordinary Scan leaked the previous archive session.** Every open creates a fresh backend session, but only a forced Refresh recorded the old one for closure — so changing roots and rescanning accumulated generation leases, mapped package files, compiled-query caches and file handles until backend shutdown. Publishing already skips the close when the reopen hands back the same session, so recording it always is safe.
- **Closing the app during Placement & Animation Studio work left it hidden but running.** The shell finds worker threads by walking the widget tree, so it waited for the Studio's baseline extraction, carry measurement and armour index — but the tab was not registered as a worker tab and exposed no shutdown surface, so nothing could ask those threads to stop and the eight-second force-stop pass never reached them. The tab now names its threads for the sweep and asks each to stop without blocking.
- **A material named `Body` was saved into a UV transform as `Körper`.** The Replacer's material chooser stores each material's key as item data, which is the localizer's signal that the visible text is a label and safe to translate — but the save path reads the visible text, not the key. Under any non-English UI the next UV edit therefore recorded a translated name, and the exported override named a material the asset does not have. `Body`, `Face`, `Default` and `Base` are all real catalog entries, so this was reachable with ordinary material names. That one chooser now opts out of item translation; the general rule is unchanged, because it is what stops Edit mode and the Source/Ghost compare view — which do compare their English text — from breaking the same way.
- **Mesh Editor's Checks and Rebuild panes stopped opening once the UI was translated.** The editor focused a report pane by comparing the tab's drawn caption to the English word, and German draws `Prüft` and `Wiederaufbau`. Validation and rebuild still ran and still filled their pane, but the view never moved, so the result read as "nothing happened". Panes are now found by the widget they were added as. The Rig button had the same fault and is fixed with it.
- **Snap, Pivot and Orient are gone from the Mesh Editor's top bar.** Nothing read them — no signal connection, no consumer anywhere in the app or the .NET helper — so picking Grid or Local changed the dropdown and nothing else. They are hidden rather than deleted, and go back on the bar when there is a real snap/pivot/orientation path to drive. Their three labels are retired from the catalogs with them.
- **Detaching a tool put its English tab caption back.** The caption was cached once when the tool registered, in English, and replayed verbatim on every detach and reattach; re-inserting a tab raises no event the localizer acts on, so nothing corrected it until the next language change. The caption is now read off the tab as it is removed.
- **Item Finder's Clear could leave an invisible filter running.** The saved category, group and material live in the dialog until the first facet response replaces them with a real selection, and the search preferred them over the controls. Pressing Clear before the facets arrived showed "All" in every control over results that were still restricted. Clear now drops the saved filter before it searches.
- **A Mesh Editor operation could finish onto the next mesh.** Closing a session cancelled its action and rebuild workers but left the request ids their completion handlers match against, and cancelling cannot recall a result already queued on the event loop. A rebuild or action started on one mesh could publish an incompatible native payload through whichever session was current by the time it landed. Both ids are retired before the cancel.
- **The Placement & Animation Studio tab kept its English name in all thirteen other languages.** Tab bars and menus read a lone `&` as "underline the next letter", so the shell doubles it before handing a tool title over — and the catalog key was recorded from the title as written, with one `&`. The drawn `Placement && Animation Studio` therefore matched nothing and fell through to the source string, in every language, while `Platzierungs- und Animationsstudio` sat in the German catalog unused. Tab text, action text and menu titles now retry the undoubled spelling and re-escape whatever comes back, so a translation carrying its own ampersand is still drawn literally. A single `&` is left alone, because that is an accelerator the caller chose rather than escaping to undo. This was the only tool title with an ampersand in it; nothing else was affected.
- **Changing the theme or moving the window to another monitor could end the app with an unhandled `QProgressBar already deleted`.** The responsive-control cache is refreshed when a tool tab is constructed and never when one of its controls is destroyed, so a panel that rebuilds its own progress bar left a dead wrapper in the list. The next appearance step walked it, and because that step runs from a timer there was no caller to catch the `RuntimeError` — it became a crash report. Dead entries are now dropped as the list is walked.

- **A Placement Studio layout guard was measuring a font that does not exist, and failed depending on what ran before it.** The offscreen test platform ships no fonts, so `QFont("Segoe UI")` resolved to an empty family and Qt answered every width from a built-in fallback roughly twice as wide as the real thing — "Distant versions" measures 208 px with no font registered and 98 px in Segoe UI at the same size. The clip-filter guard's own recorded numbers (216, 312 and 338 px) were that fallback. It then broke whenever a localization test ran first: resolving fonts for a CJK language registers those font files process-wide and never withdraws them, which halved every width in any window built afterwards. The layout tests now pin the application's own default font before measuring, so the numbers are the ones the application produces and no longer depend on test order, and a new guard registers all four CJK fonts and asserts the widths do not move. `_register_windows_cjk_fonts` records what it registered so that guard can unwind itself; the application still never withdraws a font.
- **The same guard's claim that the clip filters need two rows is withdrawn, because it is not true at the font the app uses.** `Distant versions`, `Only draws for this spot` and the scan button total 386 px at 1366x768, 412 px at 1920x1080 and 450 px at 2560x1440, inside the 620 px lane at every supported resolution. The clipping the guard was written against was real and the per-row limits still hold; only the combined-width rationale was an artefact of the fallback font. Whether the two rows should now be merged is a layout decision and is left open.
- **Count messages like "8 of 24 characters" were mistranslated in every language but German, and two of them had text from a neighbouring string spliced in.** Two signatures, both left by machine translation. `of` was read as `or`, turning a count into a disjunction — French showed `{value_0} ou {value_1} affiché`, and Russian, Japanese and both Chinese catalogs did the same on the icon-target, DDS-entry and Glow/emissive lines. Separately, `{value_1}` had been moved to sit *after* its noun, so "N of M characters" became "N of characters M" and read as if M identified the noun rather than counted it: `{value_0} de caracteres {value_1}` in both Spanish catalogs and Portuguese, `{value_0} di caratteri {value_1}` in Italian, `{value_0} из символов {value_1}` in Russian. Worst of all, the "selected vertices" line had picked up the word *bones* from the entry beside it — French read `montrant la première {value_0} des os Les sommets sélectionnés {value_1}` and Polish `pokazuje pierwsze {value_0} z wybranych kości {value_1} wierzchołki`. All twelve of the affected strings are corrected across the thirteen non-English catalogs, with the count placed against its own noun and the plural agreement fixed (French and Italian were saying "the first" in the singular for a count). The existing quality gate could not see any of this: it measures surviving *English* words, and these strings were fully translated — just wrongly.
- **The socket-name length hint spliced untranslated English into all thirteen non-English languages.** The New Socket dialog built its message by interpolating a bare `matches` / `does not match` into the middle of a sentence. A verb fragment is not a translatable unit, so it was never collected into the catalogs and appeared verbatim in German, Japanese and everywhere else — and a language that puts the verb elsewhere in the clause could not have placed it even if it had been. The dialog now formats two complete sentences, each translated as a whole.
- **Associated Assets was following references onto the wrong file, and could not find most of the formats the app already understands.** The drawer kept its own hand-written alternation of about thirty extensions while the preview pane read the shared capability manifest, which registers 141. The two disagreed about what a file points at, so a mesh, a surface, a motion, and the level and route formats could none of them be found as a companion or followed as a reference: `world/terrain.pat` is a registered mesh and was simply invisible to the drawer. Worse, the alternation ordered its branches by hand and ended without a guard, so a name was clipped onto the shorter registered format it starts with — `world/mesh.paem` read as `world/mesh.pae`, `city.paccd` as `city.pac`, and `crate.prefab_xml` as `crate.prefab`. Where a file of the clipped name existed, the drawer listed *that* file in place of the real one with full confidence, which is worse than finding nothing. The pattern, the companion suffixes and the set of formats worth decoding are now derived from the one manifest, ordered longest first, treat a dot as a path separator so a two-extension sidecar matches whole, and end on a guard; a registered format that no named rule covers falls back to its manifest role instead of landing in "Other". Tests carry each clipped target in the fixture as a decoy and prove it is present before requiring its absence from the result.
- **A Wwise bank showed none of the sounds that belong with it.** A bank names its sounds by source id, so nothing inside one reads as a path and the text scan that finds companions found nothing at all. Its media table is now read the way the preview reads it, and each id resolved to the `.wem` a loose copy carries. Associated Assets gained an `Audio / Video` group to file them under — the panel and the dialog each rendered from their own transcribed list of group headers, so a row in a group either copy omitted was computed and then silently dropped; both now render from one shared order.
- **German and European Spanish were shipping sentences that were still mostly English.** A glossary term had been substituted and the rest of the source left standing — "Anwenden fixed-size numeric edits from a CDMW HKX XML patch", "Active Malla Editor copied-source preview requires .NET/Vortice refresh". Measured over the 3,126 multi-word strings, the share where most of the English words survive was 6.8% for German and 7.6% for European Spanish against 0.4–1.1% for the other eleven; those 451 strings are now translated, and both catalogs sit at 0.3% and 0.5%. Separately, terms that are file formats in a modding tool had been translated in their everyday sense across nearly every language: `Prefab` as a prefabricated *building* (Fertighaus, Casa prefabricada, Сборный дом, Domek z prefabrykatów), `Socket XML` as an electrical outlet (Sockel, Zócalo, Prise, Presa, Розетка, 插座), and `Apply` — on the filter button — as applying *for* something (Postuler, 申请, 申し込む). `Extract Filtered` had become a noun, an extract, in five languages, and `Export Family...` in Chinese read as exporting a household through customs. Those are corrected in all thirteen, `Prefab` and `Mesh` are now declared source-identical terms alongside Turkish's existing entries, and the two strings no catalog had ever translated — the viewport's drag/pan/zoom hint and the Placement Studio parts hint — are translated everywhere.
- **Selecting a folder in the file list and extracting it produced an empty folder.** A folder export matched entries against the package-root path (`0012/ui/xml/...`), but the folder tree names entries by their archive path (`ui/xml/...`), so the prefix never matched and the export finished reporting success with nothing written. Whether the package directory is part of the path is already a field on the export request; it now selects the namespace for matching as well as for the output layout, so a folder from the tree resolves against archive paths and a folder from the Folders filter still resolves against package-root ones. Verified on the real game archive: `ui/xml/gamemain/template` exported 0 files before and its full 52 after, and `character/appearance/1_pc` exports exactly the 43 files its row claims.
- **Looking an asset up by its exact path no longer misses entries that are in the archive.** The index is ordered by an ASCII-lowercase fold of the path, but the binary search over it folded to upper case, and the two disagree wherever `_` meets a letter: uppercase puts `D` before `_`, lowercase puts `_` before `d`. Asset paths here are full of `_`, so the search regularly converged on the wrong position and reported a stored path as absent. Measured on the real game archive: of 10,240 sampled real paths, 894 — one in eleven — failed to resolve, and now none do. Item-name resolution and the item-icon lookup fell back to a basename guess when this happened, which can answer with a same-named file from another folder; preview and durable-identity lookups had no fallback and simply lost the candidate. The search also no longer decodes a whole entry record per probe, only the path it compares.
- **Changing the interface language locked the app up for half a minute, and the text then arrived in pieces.** Two independent costs. Every already-rendered string that was not a verbatim catalogue key was matched against all 2,127 templates in turn — about a millisecond each of lazy-quantifier backtracking, and one 864-character status line cost 2.9 s by itself. Templates are now bucketed by the three characters a match has to start with, or by a literal it has to contain, and a candidate is rejected by walking its literals with `str.find` before its regex ever runs; that is an exact filter, so no outcome changes. Separately, each widget that asked to be re-translated got its own deferred pass, each one re-walking its whole subtree and registering itself as another root — so realising a tool tab, which raises a show event per widget, turned into 1,454 passes over 32,949 widgets across as many turns of the event loop, which is why panels finished translating seconds apart. Requests are now drained in a single pass per turn with an ancestor absorbing its descendants. Measured offscreen on the fully realised shell (~4,450 widgets), switching to German: 6.99 s blocking plus 27.93 s of follow-on work becomes 0.58 s plus 0.06 s, of which the template matching falls from 30.88 s to 0.08 s; returning to English, 0.70 s plus 1.75 s becomes 0.44 s plus 0.04 s. The registered-root list stays at one entry instead of reaching 1,503, and the scan over it drops from 1,134,030 steps to 1.
- **Parts of the interface kept the old language after switching to another one, including back to English.** Tab labels, combo items and tree and table headers were translated twice: once by the widget that owns the text and again through its tab bar, its popup view or its header view, each keeping its own record of what the English string was. The second pass stored the *translation* as the source, so the next switch translated from it — English rendered out of French — and the doubly-translated text was wrong even in the language it was meant to be, showing "Navigateur d'archive" for `Archive Browser` and "Tous les l" for `All roles`. Each of those strings now has exactly one owner. The check for text replaced behind the localizer's back no longer treats the current language's own rendering as a new English source either, which is what left a status line German after a widget holding its own translator re-rendered it. That second record was also bypassing `_i18n_skip_combo_items`, so the language selector's own list of native language names was being translated.
- **Packaging was blocked by a stale localization manifest.** Removing the cloth physics preview took four presentation strings out of the source but left them in the generated manifest and all 14 catalogs, so `generate_ui_localization_manifest.py --check` — which runs before PyInstaller — reported the manifest stale. The manifest is regenerated and the four keys are gone from every catalog, back to 9,491 each.
- **A widget destroyed while a deferred translation pass was queued took the process down.** The weak reference kept the Python wrapper alive after its C++ object was gone, so the pass called into it and the resulting `RuntimeError` escaped an `eventFilter` override. Queued widgets are checked for validity before they are walked, and nothing in the filter can raise any more.
- **Edit Mesh: the garment refit's binding distances described whichever bind came first, not the one in force.** `Set Driver` / `Bind Selected Parts` measured every bound vertex's distance to its driver and derived the maximum, the 95th percentile, the warning threshold and the warning flag from it — once. Reset and Bake both re-anchor the refit onto the geometry in front of them, but only the anchor moved; the five numbers stayed as the original bind left them while the state contract and the "Garment:" status line went on presenting them as current. So a bake that resolved a clipping warning left it lit, and a mesh edit that pulled a garment away from its driver after binding left it dark, in both cases with a figure that no longer measured anything on screen. Rebasing now re-measures each binding against the geometry it is being anchored to and recomputes all five, including the threshold, which is derived from the driver's bounding box and therefore moves whenever the driver's shape does.
- **Edit Mesh: a refitted garment sank into the body wherever the body rotated rather than merely moved.** Morph & Refit bound each garment vertex to the closest point on a driver triangle and then displaced it by that point's translation alone — `current_point - baseline_point`, with nothing carrying the vertex's standoff from the surface. Under a pure translation that is exact, which is why a body scaled or shifted along an axis always looked right; but `taper`, `twist` and the flank of any strong region slider *rotate* the driver face, and the standoff stayed pinned to the world direction it was bound in, so cloth pushed through the body on one side and floated off it on the other. Each binding now also records its signed height along the baseline face normal, and the refit adds `height * (current normal - baseline normal)` so the standoff turns with the surface it is attached to. Only the normal component is transported: the tangential part of an edge- or corner-bound offset stays in the garment's own residual, so a hem that overhangs its driver cannot be swung around by a distant triangle, and binding a garment is still an exact no-op until a slider actually moves. Reset and Bake re-measure every binding's height against the geometry they rebase onto, so an already-baked rotation is not re-applied on the next slider move.
- **The release build failed at its last helper step whenever the .NET protocol gained a capability.** `build_pyside6_app.ps1` wrote the helper manifest from its own hand-copied transcript of `HelperBuildProvenance.RequiredProtocolCapabilities`, then started the published helper and compared the two — so `authoring_provisional_session_v1`, added on the C# side with the prewarm work, made the manifest 23 capabilities against the helper's 24 and stopped the build with ".NET Mesh Editor published provenance does not match its packaged manifest", after seventy-odd seconds of native and .NET compilation and with no indication of which field disagreed. The manifest now reads the capability list out of `HelperBuildProvenance.cs` and the version out of the `.csproj`, so the two can no longer drift, and a mismatch names every field that differs and lists the capabilities each side has that the other does not. The contract is also read in the first seconds of a build rather than only after the compile, so a declaration the script cannot parse stops it immediately.
- **A build blocked by the running app reported it as a permissions problem.** Publishing into `dist/` fails with "Access to the path ... is denied" when the portable exe is open, which reads as an ACL or antivirus fault and sends the reader looking in the wrong place — after five minutes of building, at the last step. The retry loops now name the processes running out of the path they could not replace, and say to close it.
- **The guard that was supposed to cover the helper manifest required the bug.** `test_release_packaging.py` asserted that nine named capabilities appear verbatim in `build_pyside6_app.ps1` — a check that can only pass while the duplication exists, and one carrying its own third copy of the list, three capabilities short. It was green throughout. It now asserts the derivation instead, and `tests/test_dotnet_helper_manifest_contract.py` runs the build script's own contract function against the .NET sources, in half a second, under the `mesh-unit` gate. Verified against a reshaped C# declaration and against a re-hardcoded list: it fails on both and names the fix.
- **"Solid (Textured)" was reported as succeeding without ever compiling a texture.** The resident helper launches with a deliberately textureless package — every submesh carries `"reason": "textures_on_demand"`, the manifest ships zero resources, and base falls back to `neutral_checker` — while the host seeds `standalone_dotnet_material_signature` from that same package and all three material generations start at zero. So on an unedited mesh the first textured request computed the very same signature, `generation <= completed` held as `0 <= 0`, and both the publish path and the resolve path took their "the helper already holds exactly these materials" shortcut. Nothing was compiled, nothing was sent, no acknowledgement was expected and no failure was logged — the viewport was simply told to show `textured` over a package with no material resources, so `MaterialHasBase` stayed 0 and Solid (Textured) drew exactly like Faces (No Textures), Solid + Wire exactly like Faces + Wire. Both shortcuts now also require that a material generation was actually applied, which is the condition the display-mode handler already used and these two paths did not.
- **X-Ray applied to one pane only.** `context.XRay` had exactly two writers — `TrySetDisplayMode` and `SaveActivePresentationContext` — and both touch the active pane's context alone; it was the one piece of display state missing from `SynchronizePresentationDisplaySettings`, the only fan-out to every context. Since each pane renders from its own context, picking X-Ray changed whichever pane happened to be active, the Original/reference one, and left the other still depth-testing its overlays. X-Ray is derived by the same switch that sets the display mode, so it now travels with it.
- **A host-driven display-mode change reached one pane; the helper's own combo reached all of them.** `viewport_display_update` — the route the Edit Mesh tool rail drives — called the bare `TrySetDisplayMode`, while the helper's internal Preview mode combo called `TrySetSynchronizedDisplayMode`. The host route uses the synchronized setter too now, so the two controls can no longer disagree about what the viewport is showing.
- **Mesh View's "Solid (Textured)" loaded no textures, and "Solid + Wire" would not stay selected.** Both symptoms were one abort. A normal or height map whose raw `.dds` is packaged verbatim for the resident viewport is deliberately never decoded to a preview PNG, because the combiner's own output for that channel is discarded anyway — but the combiner still walked the input, could not open a `.dds` through `QImageReader`, and recorded `normal unreadable:<name>.dds`. The material compiler treats any `unreadable:` note as a hard blocker, so the whole compile failed and no textures ever reached the viewport, for every mesh carrying a raw normal map. The controls were telling the truth about that: a textured mode that cannot be entered snaps back to its untextured fallback, which is why picking Solid + Wire left "Faces + Wire" showing. A skipped decode is now labelled as one, and only a genuine read failure still blocks.
- **The display-mode gate only ever drove four of the eight modes it ships.** `Faces + Wire`, `Solid + Wire`, `Wire` and `X-Ray` were never asked for, never acknowledged and never captured, so "Solid + Wire" could reach users broken behind a green harness run. All eight are exercised against a real `.pac` now, each checked against the viewport's own flag table and against draw counters that must advance, and the harness table is pinned to the combo table so a mode added to the UI fails the guard until it is covered.
- **Opening the Mesh Editor showed the editor assembling itself, then loading.** The embedded helper's window was revealed at the end of its startup realization — before the texture load it starts on the very next line, and therefore seconds before the first drawn frame. The reader got a fully built but empty editor: panels, rail and splitters painted by WinForms over a viewport with nothing in it, then the model arriving separately. That reveal could not simply be moved later, because the D3D11 device was created by the first paint, a hidden window never gets one, and `TryApplyMaterialState` fails outright without a device — so the reveal *had* to precede the textures for the textures to bind at all. The renderer is now brought up explicitly while the window is still hidden (`EnsureRendererInitialized`, which needs the handle, not visibility), and the reveal moves to the moment geometry, materials and textures are all bound. Measured against the real helper, the window used to appear ~3.5 s before `ready` and now appears 0.8 ms after `textures_ready`. It cannot wait for the frame itself — the frame is produced by a paint — so this is as late as it goes. Every terminal texture failure reveals too, or an error would be reported into a status line behind the host's spinner.
- **The Mesh Editor's helper could not be warmed until the moment it was needed.** The resident architecture was already there — one helper process, scenes hot-swapped into it, hidden rather than killed on exit — but the *first* open per edit session still paid a cold process start, JIT and D3D device creation, because the prewarm could only begin once the authoritative edit-session id existed. That ordering was load-bearing: an authoring handshake made before the session existed latched a throwaway id, and both sides then refused the real one (`session_mismatch` in the helper, "authoring session changed while the resident helper was active" in the host). A prewarm handshake is now marked provisional and may be adopted exactly once, by exactly one real session, before it has owned any mutation; a real session can still never be displaced. The alignment preview shell starts the helper 750 ms after it opens, so the start-up is spent while the user is still choosing what to edit. The rebind is gated on the helper advertising `authoring_provisional_session_v1` rather than required, so an older helper still runs — and a prewarm holding a session it cannot hand back is dropped rather than being allowed to refuse a real one.
- **The Mesh Editor's layout re-asserted itself on screen every time it opened.** The helper emits `activated` *after* it has revealed its window, and the host answered that by pushing session, scene and presentation state — including the interaction mode. A mesh-edit interaction frame makes the helper re-run `ApplyInteractionModeControls`, which re-asserts the tool rail and re-parents every section, so the reader watched the editor rearrange itself immediately after it appeared. The state was already correct: `_rehydrate_shared_dotnet_controller` sends exactly the same three frames from inside the package-apply path, which runs *before* activation and therefore while the window is still hidden. The `activated` handler now skips the push when the rehydrator has already delivered it for the current process generation — a suspend/resume, which activates without a package apply and so has no rehydrate behind it, still sends its state.
- **A prewarmed helper flashed its placeholder scene into the pane before the real model.** Moving the prewarm earlier exposed a stage that had been harmless while it only ever ran at click time: the helper revealed its window at the end of its own start-up regardless of *what* it was showing, and a prewarm is showing a procedural placeholder nobody asked to see. Measured on the wire, the window was revealed, painted, presented, and hidden again 67 ms later when the host answered `ready` with `deactivate_request` — a flash of the wrong model at dialog-open time, and a flicker the earlier reveal fix did not cover because it was never a startup-ordering problem. A prewarm launch is flagged (`--prewarm`) and stays hidden throughout; the host never needed `ready` from one, because it warms the GPU with an offscreen capture instead. The window is revealed by the `activate_request` that follows a real package, 15 ms after `package_load_applied` — by which point geometry, materials and textures are all bound, so the first frame the pane ever shows is the real model. With the process and device already warm, that whole path now takes 33 ms from request to revealed.
- **Every tool rail page in the Mesh Editor said its own name twice, and the Move page said two different names.** The tool dock header already names the page, so the section box beneath it repeated it verbatim — "SELECTION" over a "SELECTION" group, and the same for Brush, Topo, Colour and Morph. Worse on Move, where the header read "TRANSFORM" while the rail button, the tool it activates and the status line all say Move. The header is Move now, and the section a rail page is built around drops its own caption. Part Pick keeps its caption, because it is the second box on the Selection page, as do the scene groups on the right, which have no header of their own. The classic layout has no dock header, so it gets every caption back.
- **Dragging the Mesh Editor's placement Gizmo tore the window apart and left the mesh behind.** Every `placement_transform_request` — one per pointer sample, so roughly one every 30 ms — was answered with a full transform update: an authoritative scene frame recalculated over every vertex on a worker, published to the resident helper, and a static preview rebuild armed behind it. Each of those frames made the helper re-run `ApplyInteractionModeControls`, which in placement mode restores the classic layout: a redraw batch over the whole form, every tool section cleared and re-added, both flanks collapsed and re-revealed. At drag cadence that is the flicker, and it reads as the Edit Mesh layout because that restore is the same code path leaving Edit Mesh takes. Intermediate samples now only move the spin boxes and their sliders; the terminal sample publishes the frame that makes the placement authoritative again, and the helper re-asserts its interaction-mode controls only when the mode actually changed — the guard a package swap already used.
- **A gizmo drag stopped following the pointer once an authoritative frame landed mid-drag.** The drag renders from a provisional placement snapshot taken at mouse-down, and a frame whose placement matched cleared that snapshot. If the pointer happened to be at rest when one arrived — the common case, because the frame echoes a sample the pointer has already reached — the next drag sample re-took the snapshot, pairing the already-moved translation with the pre-move model matrix. From then on the mesh sat at the echoed sample and every further movement was measured from a stale base. A live drag owns its snapshot until it ends now, and a drag left open by a lost mouse-up is closed on the next pointer move rather than stalling every later frame.
- **Unticking Gizmo (or Grid) came undone on the next scene update.** Overlay visibility belongs to the two checkboxes and arrives through presentation updates, but every authoritative scene frame writes `"visible": true` for both and the helper adopted it — so switching an overlay off held until anything republished the scene, such as nudging a Location value. Both flags are host state now and survive an incoming frame, the same way the resident world grid's origin and spacing already did.
- **"Use This as Swap Source" did nothing on the v2 backend, which is the normal way the swap is used.** Prepared dependency snapshots are held in a four-slot LRU, and choosing a swap source means previewing other files — so arming a target and then browsing to find its source evicted the target's own snapshot within four previews. `archive_workflow_dependency_context` then raised, and `_start_archive_in_game_mesh_swap` reported it through `set_status_message`, which renders on the Textures tab: from Archive Browser the click did nothing and said nothing, the same shape as the arming bug one step earlier. The armed target's snapshot is pinned against eviction for as long as the swap is armed, including across the `clear_snapshot` a scope change performs, and both failure paths now raise a dialog naming the target, the source, and the reason. Found from a real session log that showed the target previewed, then four other files previewed, then a click that produced no event at all.
- **The swap banner now says when its target is not ready yet.** Arming can beat the asynchronous dependency preparation, and a pin recorded before the snapshot exists only takes effect once it lands — so there was a window where the banner promised a swap that would have failed. The pin is applied from the banner refresh, which keeps the two from ever disagreeing, and the banner reads "Still preparing this target's dependencies" until the snapshot arrives; the ready handler refreshes it so the message clears by itself.
- **An in-game mesh swap from a cloth-bearing donor crashed before the scope dialog appeared.** `contract_warning` was built inside the `source_has_larger_material_contract` branch and then used one level out, under `preserve_source_contract_default` — which is also true when the donor merely carries cloth/PBD. A weapon donor with a tassel or banner but no more material wrappers than its target therefore raised `UnboundLocalError` on the way to the dialog, so the swap did nothing and said nothing. The warning is built whenever the contract is detected now.
- **Starting an in-game mesh swap looked like it had done nothing.** Arming a target wrote its confirmation to `set_status_message`, which lands on the Textures tab's progress panel — not visible from Archive Browser — and otherwise only changed the label of a menu item inside the collapsed "Mesh Edit" dropdown. The only way to discover you had armed anything was to right-click the same file again and read "Cancel In-Game Mesh Swap Target". Archive Browser now carries a banner above the file list naming the target and offering one-click Cancel, and it survives filtering, because filtering to find the source is the next thing you do.
- **Arming a swap from the action button jumped to the Mesh Editor first.** `_swap_current_archive_mesh_with_in_game` opened the editor before setting the target, so the button moved you away from the browser you needed to pick the source in. Both the button and the Mesh Editor's own In-Game Swap now arm and stay put — or return to Archive Browser — and the editor opens once both sides are known, which also stops a redundant `mesh_edit` frame being pushed at the tool rail.
- **A donor's cloth/PBD contract was detected and then discarded.** Complete In-Game Swap regenerates the target material sidecar from the alignment mapping, and nothing in that rebuild carries `_pbdSimulationMaterialName` or `_physicsFileName` across — both are read for reporting only. The preflight already detected the donor contract, but `complete_swap_scope_default` was hardcoded to `True`, so the detection changed a warning label and nothing else. A donor with cloth or a wider material contract now defaults to Select Item Family, which copies the source sidecar onto the target sidecar path, and its physics rows are preselected even across weapon folders, because a copied sidecar names them.
- **Half the in-game swap scope table's explanations never rendered.** The dialog looked its relationship edges up by a `"pamt::path"` string while the preflight keyed them by `ArchiveEntryIdentity`, so every lookup missed: the "What this row means" column fell back to generic heuristics and the graph-derived default checks never applied. Both sides key on `ArchiveEntryIdentity` now, which also drops a `Path.resolve()` per row that the old string key needed.
- **The swap scope dialog now warns when a checked row has no target file.** Retargeting a source companion to a target that has no counterpart falls through to a string-substituted guess, and a payload written there is referenced by nothing the game loads. That is the normal case for physics when the target has none of its own, and it silently produced a mod that did nothing.
- **Unticking "Load textures" did nothing against a textured model.** `_archive_textures_visible` mirrors what the renderer is showing, and the two desync on the very first load: the package load asks for `untextured_wire` and records `False`, while the renderer settles on `textured` because the package carries texture resources. The hide branch was gated on that mirror being `True`, so the display mode was never sent at all — which is why fixing the renderer's package-swap default and the silent rejection did not help. It is stated on every toggle now rather than trusted from a local belief about what the viewport already shows.
- **The Archive Browser preview refused every display change the host sent it.** Loading a simple-preview package makes the renderer latch that package's scene session id — `archive_preview` — as its resident session, and it then compares every correlated message against it by ordinal equality. Adopting a package's session id was wired only for the authoring profile, because an edit session owns the helper and must not be stolen; the preview profile kept the random UUID it generates at construction, so `AcceptMaterialSession` refused the lot. Unticking "Load textures" sent a correct `untextured_wire` that was rejected on arrival, which is why fixing the host's decision path three times changed nothing. A preview owns nothing, so it now follows the package.
- **A submesh that shares a material with another submesh rendered as a flat tint instead of its texture.** A base texture derived by convention from the material's normal map is registered against the first wrapper that declared it, and base selection then dropped any candidate whose wrapper index was not the mesh's own local index. The support roles were given an exemption for this in the past — a binding naming the mesh's own material — and base never got one, so the second submesh kept its normal, surface and height maps and lost only its albedo. On `cd_phm_02_sword_0013`, `cd_phm_02_acc_0032` is bound to two accessory submeshes and only the first was textured; on `cd_m0001_00_geumgangbulgwe_nude_0001` the second head submesh was picking up a `cd_pgm_` texture from another asset family entirely.
- Sharing a material *name* is not enough to make two wrappers' textures interchangeable, so the exemption is narrower than the support-role one: the candidate's own texture family also has to be the mesh's material. `cd_m0041_00_woodenowl_00_0001` is why — its Eye and Body submeshes both declare `CD_M0041_00_WoodenOwl_0001` yet bind `blackoil` and the owl's own atlas, and the looser rule let the Eye's texture reach five other batches. Across a 40-asset sample, 105 of 107 batch selections are unchanged, two improve, none are lost.
- **A rejected display change was invisible in the Archive Browser.** Only the Mesh Editor read `presentation_state_update_ack`, so when the renderer refused an update — a stale session or process generation — the toolbar showed the new state over a viewport that had not changed, with no message anywhere. This is the shape of "unticking Load textures does nothing". The Archive Browser reports the refusal and its reason now.
- **A package swap put textures back after they had been turned off.** `ReplaceResidentPackage` settled on the package's own default mode, which is `textured` whenever the package carries texture resources, discarding whatever the host had last asked for. Once the host has named a display mode it owns the choice and a swap keeps it; a package with no textures still falls back to the untextured equivalent rather than a mode it cannot honour.
- **Changing the .NET/Vortice cache mode pruned only half the cache, and Clear Preview Cache cleared only half of it.** Durable preview packages live in two tiers under `preview/models`: `packages`, holding the decoded output, and `dotnet_vortice/packages`, holding the Vortice-ready packages the resident renderer actually loads. Both are bounded independently at store time, but prune and clear walked only the first, because each took a single cache root and appended `packages` to it. Dropping Aggressive to Balanced left the derived tier at its old size, and Clear Preview Cache reported that it had removed "durable .NET/Vortice preview packages" while leaving the tier named after them on disk. `prune`/`clear` now take every tier, and the derived root has one accessor rather than four hardcoded `"dotnet_vortice"` literals that could drift apart again.
- **Setting the cache to Off reclaimed nothing.** Off means a zero budget, and a zero budget makes the prune return before it looks at anything, so choosing "Off (least disk use)" left every byte an earlier mode had written. It clears the durable tiers now; packages pinned by a live preview are still skipped.
- **A queued preview prewarm could take the whole app down.** `_DotNetPreviewPrewarmTask` runs on the *global* thread pool and nothing waits for it, so it can outlive the host that started it. `self.signals` is then a deleted QObject and the emit raises `Internal C++ object already deleted` — and that emit sat outside the guard, so the exception escaped `QRunnable.run()`. An exception escaping a Python override of a C++ virtual is fatal: PySide6 reports `Error calling Python override of QRunnable::run()` and the process dies. In the app that means closing a preview while a prewarm is in flight; in CI it killed whichever test happened to be running when the task came due, which is how it presented as a crash in the mesh builder tests at 24%.
- A prewarm is best-effort by definition, so nothing in it is worth a crash: `run()` now contains its own failures and treats a departed host as nobody left to tell.

- **The Translation Studio tests built a whole tab per test and destroyed none of them**, so the widget trees accumulated across the class and the interpreter died part-way through that file at 98% of a full run, on 3.14 only, while 3.11 finished clean with 7,700 passing. The tabs are closed and their deletion processed now. This is the fourth instance of one pattern this evening, after the preview session controller, the placement studio window, and the archive backend client: a Qt object built with no parent and left for the collector, destroyed later inside an unrelated test's Qt work, aborting the process with no traceback and no summary. Widgets need `close()` then `deleteLater()` then a `processEvents()` pass; forcing the deferred delete instead destroys them mid-teardown and crashes.

- **A finished Mesh Editor action could leave a progress message as the last word on the status line.** Progress arrives on a queued cross-thread signal, so one emitted just before the result can be delivered just after it, and the handler only checked that the request id was current, never that the request had already finished. The status line then read `Applied Rotate.` where the completion handler had just written the session state, revision and undo depth. Each terminal handler records the request as finished now and progress for it is ignored. Found because it failed CI on one interpreter leg and not the other, which is exactly the shape a delivery-order race takes.

- **Opening Translation Studio took 3.6 seconds, and pressing Load paid the same 3.6 seconds again.** Both went to one question — which package holds this language? — answered by parsing all 33 package tables on the UI thread, inside the tab's constructor. Almost all of it was waste: the fourteen `.paloc` tables live in packages 0019–0032, which hold **one entry each** and parse in under a millisecond, while the time goes to 0000, 0004 and 0009, which carry 200,000–400,000 entries apiece and not one localization string between them.
- Nothing can know that without parsing, so the new `language_index.py` parses once and caches the answer against the package files' own sizes and modification times. Warm, listing the languages is one `stat` per package and `read_language` parses the single one-entry table that actually holds it; cold, the sweep runs on a worker and the tab opens immediately. Measured on the installed game: **constructor 3.7 s → 0.14 s, Load 3.6 s → 0.69 s** including the 16 MB parse and a reference language. The cache is derived and always safe to delete — a stale one is never used, because the fingerprint has to match first.
- Moving that sweep onto a worker introduced a crash worth recording. A `QThread` parented to the tab is destroyed with the tab, and destroying a *running* one is an access violation — exactly what closing or reloading the tab mid-sweep would do. It surfaced as `Windows fatal exception: access violation` in the test run rather than in the panel. The worker threads are unparented and held in a module-level set until they finish; Qt severs the signal to the dead receiver on its own.
